CAPITAL" CHAT.

During two or three recent mayoralties the Lord Mayor for the time being has thrown open the Mansion House to 1,500 or 1,600 friends for the enjoyment of what has been called a "Rational Sunday," the rationalness consisting in listening to a concert of sacred music. The present Lord Mayor, on the very eve of his retirement, had his "Rational Sunday." It was given in the Egyptian Hall of the Mansion House, and the National Sun-' day League was greatly in evidence, one of the aldermen of the City being its president. Coming on the back of the recent throwing open of the Guildhall pictures on Sunday, the Rational Sunday concert shows the direction in which the minds of City men are moving on the Sunday question. But a similar tendency to "rationalise" the Sunday, as it is called, is abroad all over London, and in an- other generation the old-fashioned Sabbath day will evidently be a thing entirely of the past, at least in the metropolis.

Two points of great interest on this subject are much exercising men's minds now. One of these is whether the Conservative leaders will treat the at- tack on the House of Lords by a blank and uncom- promising negative or by stating an alternative scheme of reform calculated to make the Second Chamber far more powerful than now. The other is as to the possibility of the Government living through another session in its endeavour either to carry certain pressing measures or to throw on the Lords the added odium of rejecting them. I find,in some quarters a disposition to think that a know- ledge of the issue to be fought out at the polling booths will demoralise the House of Commons for legislative purposes, and that the force of circum- stances will hurry the crisis on beyond the control of Ministers. But one thing insisted on is that we must have a new and enlightened Registration Ant as the essential condition of referring the future of the House of Lords to the constituencies.

The cue of the Tory Press is to endeavour to ridicule Lord Rosebery's utterance as a very little thing of the mouse produced by the labouring mountain order. This attempt to laugh away the flag unfurled at Bradford as an exceedingly insig- nificant rag is too transparent to deceive anyone. What, it is asked, would have satisfied these fire- eaters if they think procedure by resolution— which was at any rate effectual in 1860 and at other times—a mere discharge of empty cartridge? There wonld be considerable surprise if, when Lord Salisbury comes to speak, he should be found to treat the gauntlet thrown down to him as a thin kid glove, meant for appearance and not for use, for whatever ingenuity may be displayed in mic- roscopically searching individual passages in Lord Rosebery's speech for indications of hesitancy,those who build upon his recognition of difficulties as indicating a want of thoroughness are founding their hopes on sand. The one thing certain is that Lord Rosebery and his Cabinet are in earnest in determining that the country shall once for all say aye or nay to the question—" Shall the House of Lords continue?" They have put t';eir hands to the plough, and will not look back. cess

When the Prim of Wales arrived in London on Monday afternoon it struck observers that she looked very pale. She was dressed in the deepest black—what" for, no one knew—as were also her daughters Princesses Victoria and Maud. There was not a hit of colour about the Princesses. It is curious. The Prince of Wales was with them, and he likewise was in black, as were all the suite. The party is to stay in town till Friday, when, if the Czar's improvement continues, they are to go to Sandringham for the birthday of the Prince, when parties are to be held. This visit is primarily for shopping purposes. The Duke of York came up with hit parents and went on to York House, St. James's to join the Duchess, who has been there with Prince Edward since Friday last week.

It is to be hoped that two such Labour leaders as Mr. John Burns and Mr. George Shipton will not carry their quarrel too far; but it is a significant ' illustration of the bitterness with which the Em- pire Music gall controversy has entered into our civic life that two such men have taken to libelling each other. Mr. Shipton is secretary of the Theatri- cal Operatives' Union, and on behalf of the work- people whom the closing of the theatre would throw out of employment he opposed Mr. Chant's crusade. Mr. Burns throws himself into the support of purity,but kept his opinions to himself until the county council discussed the question, when, in'the course of his speech, he accused Mr. Shipton, in strong and outspoken language, of unduly interfer- ing in the matter. Mr. Shipton, confident of the legality of all he has done, is meditating bringing the law of libel into force againse Mr. Burns unless some candid friend advises him not to be too ridicu- lous. The interests of labour, which both have so much at heart, will not be forwarded by two of labour's typical representatives falling foul of each over the Empire promenade.

The creature comforts of the Swazi Indunas are being looked after appropriately enough by Mr. 1 Whiteley, the universal provider. Including the warrior seven feet high, who has left behind in his home tokens of his prowess in the shape of dried visages of slain foes, they are housed at Hammer- smith. There was a funny scene on Tuesday when the Indunas were taken to be fitted with or measured for clothes of European cut. The sight of seven Indunas with stone rings on their heads try- ing on shining top hats and the latest thing in frock coats was too comic for any psges but those of "Punch." Though much perplexed what to do with their coat tails and puzzled by trousers, they yet suffer these with resignation. But they draw the line at patent leather boots, and absolutely refuse to wear them, even though four sizes larger than any usually kept in stock. When these warriors appear in the parks clad in the garb of Pall Mall, but shoeless and sockless, may I be there to see! To our tobacco they take kindly, with pre- ference for the stronger brands, hut they show I quite a Good Templar's distaste for intoxicating drinks.

Mr. Stuart, the interpreter who accompanies this Swaziland deputation, is already occupied with arranging for the presentation of the views of the Indunas to those in authority. There is no doubt that among both English and natives in Swaziland the prospect of having the country handed over to the Boers is regarded with vast dislike. The Boer would harry both white and black, especially if in toossession of land which he wishes for cattle grazing. A friend of mine, a former Liverpool journalist who is now farming in Swaziland, when over in this country a couple of years ago, des- cribed the conduct of the Boers as utterly unbear- able wherever they had the least authority. The country is very tine, full of excellent farm land,and in parts a good game country, which it would be a pity to see pass away from us.

The ninth annual exhibition of the London and Provincial Ornithological Society which was held this week, was certainly an excellent show. There were over a thousand entries, and in the canaries alone there were 94 classes. In fact, the students of canaries—one learns with some interest that these are both professionals and amateurs—can examine the finest varieties. Under artificial cul- ture canaries are becoming almost as much special- ised as pigeons; but luckily—one fears it is not yet ' been made as ugly and unnatural as some pigeon monstrosities. It is rather shocking to see that there is a class for migratory birds. The migratory instinct is extraordinarily strong, and to put a swallow or a nightingale in a cage and so force it to withstand the darkness and cold of an English winter is to subject the bird to what is probably a peculiarly painful death. Some birds can stand cage life; but to put genuine migrants into cages is positively cruel. Among curiosities might be noticed an albino twite, or mountain linnet. This bird, by the way, takes comfortably to cage life though it is a sub-Arctic species and must not be confined to warm rooms. Its pretty little conver- sation, " Mrs. Wolseley,Mrs. Wolseley, how d'ye do ?" is quite charming. The foreign classes are large, and fairly good as regards variety. A monkey,who nevertheless joins to the best of his small ability in the singing, would seem to be wrongly classified in an ornithological show.

One man was killed and several injured on Wednesday by a fall of timber at the Salford Docks.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY NOVEMBER 3, 1894.

A greater portion of divorces takes place between the fifth and tenth year of married life.

_ There is to be a joint celebration of the golden wedding of the Earl of Bradford, the silver wedding of his son, Viscount Newport, and the coming of age of the latter's eldest son.

Admiral Ito, the Japanese hero of the day, who is known among his devoted sailors as "the Lean Ad- miral," on account of his extreme thinness, has a daugh- ter who is oie of Japan's very few blonde beauties. If a train, moving at the speed sf twenty-five miles an hour, were suddenly stopped, a passenger would experience a shock equal to that of falling from a second floor window; at thirty miles an hour, he might as well fall from a height of three pairs of stairs; and an express train's sudden stoppage would be like a fall from a fourth storey.

It has been estimated from the stamp duties paid by patent medicine makers that four million of pills are taken by the inhabitants of the United Kingdom every week. In France the quantity is about half. Only about one million are taken by the people of Russia. The Australians are the biggest pill takers in the world.

Miss Adeline Sergeant, the novelist, has her home in some residential chambers near Tottenham Court-road. Her " flat " is about the prettiest in a building which is full of artistic taste and refinement. Miss Sergeant held a scholarship at Queen's College, London, worked for some time as a journalist in Dundee, and has published about a dozen novels. She takes considerable interest in social questions, is a Fabian, and works in the Board Sctools of St. Pancras as one of the Committee of Local Managers. In a letter to a Continental journal, Mr. Astrup, the Norwegian explorer, in speaking of the recent Arctic expedition manned by Lieutenant Peary, mentions that the relations between members of the party became somewhat strained en route, and that "the ladies had an extremely bad influence on the good relations and co-operation among the expedition.' We conclude,says the "Lady," that "armed neutrality" was by no means their attitude. This is strange, for if "freezing polite- ness " were to be found anywhere, one would expect to find it in these Northern latitudes. While Lord Salisbury never wears a buttonhole, Mr. Gladstone, is generally to be seen attached to a laree nosegay, says " Pearson's." Mrs. Gladstone usually purchases the flowers when they are travelling together abroad, but at home the ex-Premier makes his own choice. It is a mistake, by the way, to suppose that Mr. Gladstone never smokes. The G.0.M., however draws the line at cigarettes, only accepting cigars from the Prince of Wales ani a few favourite hosts. Person- ally, he would rather leave tobacco in every form, alone.

Dining Rules. Fruit is not to be bitten; it should be peeled and cut with a fruit knife.

Never drum with the fingers upon the tablf"—or with the feet upon the floor.

No gentleman will ever place his arms upon the table either before, during,or after a meal. Meats are to be cut with a single gliding movement of the knife, not by converting it into a saw. Keep the elbows always close to the side, no matter how ample may be the room between guests. Be punctual—to keep a dinner party waiting under any circumstances is the gravest social indecorum. Take soup only from the side of the spoon—unless wearing a moustache; never sip it with an audible sound.

Never play with knife and fork or other table utensils; do not touch them at all, except when about to use them.

Fish is to be taken with a fork only; it should be carried to the mouth with the tines of the fork pointing downward.

Do not forget that cheerfulness" suggests good health, a clear conscience, and a soul at peace with all human nature." Food.

It has heen demonstrated over and over again that man cannot live on a diet composed of any onc of the alimentary principles. Whenever the attempt has been made to support life on any of them, such as albumen alone, or sugar or starch alone, or simple gelatine, the result has been a practical starvation. An experimenter managed to support lite for ten days on a diet composed entirely of .albumen, derived from bullock's bleod and water. Symptoms of an alarming nature soon showed themselves' and at the end of ten days he was obligcd to desist. Attempts of a less rigid kind also terminated in a similar way ; and in those cases where life had been supported entirely upon lean meat or upon cheese the health has quickly deteriorated. In some conditions of the constitution, such as diabetes, it becomes necessary to deprive the patient of carbo- hydrates. because of his inability to assimilate them ; and enly those who have themselves suffered or have closely watched the sufferings of others can be aware of the misery which a restricted diet of this kind entails on the sufferer, and the terrible longing for the forbidden food. In the early days of his existence man is dependent entirely on one article of diet, viz., milk ; and it is a remarkable and most instructive fact that milk contains an admixture of all the alimentary princi- ples we have enumerated—the albuminates, carbo- hydrates, fat, water, and salts. Milk may, therefore, be regarded as a typical food. If milk be allowed to stand, the cream rises to the top, and may be skimmed off. This is the fatty constituent of milk. If to the skimmed milk Some rennet be added ,the casein will coagulate,and can be separated as curd. This is the albuminate or nitrogenous constituent of milk. The fluid left after the coagulation of the casein, which is known as whey, con- tains the sugar of milk, or lactin, which is the carbo- hydrate constituent, as well as the salts and most of the water. Roughly speaking, therefore, butter is almost pure fat, cheese is almost purely albuminous. and whey is the vehicle of the sugar and salts.—" The Family Physician.

THE DOUBLE LIFE: 08, THE HAMPTON MYSTERYi By the Author of " DAAawoOD.'9

CHAPTER XXXII: THE EVE OF A DISCOVERY. Ido commend me to you. HAMLET. The Burnt Downs, as has been said, comprises a barren and desolate region of some extent, thickly covered with furze bushes, and furrowed with many fissures and inequalities. There are no trees on this prairie-like expanse, and but few flowers grow here besides the yellow blossoms of the gorse. Its chief inhabitants are the flocks of sheep which feed on the succulent herbage, and the wild hares which live and thrive under cover of the bushes. In the midst of this dreary region upon a high knoll commanding a wide view, stood a small, neat, cobble-stone cottage, with two rooms, and a great, old-fashioned, projecting chimney. This dwelling was the home of Peggy Willsey, the woman who had loved the unworthy George Negwyn enough to remain single for his sake. She lived alone here, tending her sheep, which fed on the Downs and finding a strange pleasure in the utter solitude. She was not devoid of womanly tastes, as was evidenced by the wild rose bushes which had been carefully trained over the small, leaden-sashed windows and over the low, latticed porch. These vines were leafless now, but under the windows a few early flowers were unfolding their petals, des- pite the yet chilly atmosphere. In the rear of the tiny dwelling was a still smaller stable, built of cobble-stones, with a thatched roof. Here were housed a small stout donkey and a cow, when the weather did not per- mit them to wander on the Downs. On the morning subsequent to the maiden's flight from Trevelyan Park, soon after sunrise, Giralda and Mrs. Pumpton entered on the Downs, ,guid- ing their horses in the path leading to the cottage, Both were fatigued beyond measure. Their tide had been long and hard, rind not without its perils. They had encountered few people, only a farmer returning home about midnight in his wagon, and, since daybreak, two or three labourers going to their work; yet they had not been able to divest themselves of a nervous sense of danger ,and pursuit. "You look tired out, Miss," said Mrs. Pumpton, compassionately regarding the pale face of her young charge. "Your excitement, sleeplessness, and this hard night's ride, have, all together, been too much for you. But we shall soon be at Peg- gy's now. You can see her cottage from here." She pointed toward it, and Giralda's gaze fol- lowed the direction of her outstretched finger. The young girl brightened a little at the near prospect of shelter and rest. "I shall feel safe here, "she said, smiling faint- ly. "My enemies will never find me here while I an waiting for a letter from mamma. Do you think that this Peggy Willsey will receive me ? "I know she will," was the quick response. "Peggy thinks the Trevelyans the greatest family in the world. There is nothing she would not do for a Trevelyan. She remembers. Mr. Geoffrey well. Many's the gay smile and pleasant word he gave her when he was at the Park. He had a smile and a kind word for everybody, Miss, and everbody loved him. Peggy will be your friend, and stand by you through thick and thin, if you tell her that you are Mr. Geoffrey's daughter l' By this time the travellers had approached the cottage sufficiently near to see that the door was open. A line smoke was curling up from the chimney, and it was evident that breakfast was in course of preparation. "That's odd," said Mrs. Pumpton, urging her horse into a gallop. "Peggy was alway used to breakfast here at daybreak. What's set her into this lazy habit, I should like to know. At any rate, Miss, we're just in time." With an expression of complete satisfaction, the good woman urged her horse to still greater speed, Giralda following her example. As they neared tho cottage they saw a woman's form appear in the door-way and a startled face looked out at them from the shadow of the bare rose-vines, and then face and form disappeared, and the sound of a door hurriedly shut followed. A minute later they rode up to the porch and dismounted. They had scarcely gained their feet when the woman they had before seen appeared in the door-way, a slightly perturbed expression on her features. She was a tall, large; masculine looking woman with an arm and a step like a grenadier. Her large, full-moon face had an honest look that won Ciralda's liking in an instant. Her eyes had a kindly look in them, and her mouth wore an ex- pression at once pleasant and shrewd. Her dress of brown stuff was kilted about her waist, display- ing a pair of heavy feet, encased in stout boots yet wet with the dew of a long morning tramp on the Downs. This was Peggy Willsey. She recognized the housekeeper of Trevelyan Park at a glance, and sprang forward with outstretched hand and a dozen eager questions on her lips. "Come in, come in" she exclaimed, hospitably, after pausing to tether the tired horses. "And the lady too 1" she added, with a cour- tesy. "My poor cottage is quite at your service 1" She led the way into the dwelling. It was as neat in the interior as the exterior. The door of the inner room was closed, but the main room had an air of simple comfort to which English cottages of the humbler type are in general utter strangers. Peggy, however, was the daugh- ter of a small farmer, and her ideas had been culti- vated by years of service at Trevelyan Park. Being independent on her small means, it was scarcely to be wondered at that she had curtains of white dimity at the windows, a bright, home-made carpet on the floor, a sampler of mournful design and duly framed on the clean, whitewashed wall, and a red, chintz-covered lounge with square pillows in the coziest corner of the room. But the feature which most attracted the worthy Mrs. Pumptori was the fire on the hearth, with tea- kettle singing busily on its crane, and a delicate bird broiling over the coals. "A bird on toast !" she muttered. "A dish for a lord, or a sick person! You used to live on course food, Peggy. Have you turned dainty all at once?" Peggy colored, and murmured something her guests did not understand, as she hastened to place chairs for them. "Can't you give us something to eat at once?" questioned the housekeeper, as Giralda sat down, tired and pallid on the lounge. "This breakfast of yours will just do for my young lady, Peggy." The hostess looked embarrassed, but a glance at Giralda compelled her to assent. "I've more birds," she said, hastening to produce them from her small larder. "Whatever brought you to see me, Mrs. Pumpton? How long are you thinking of staying ?" These straightforward questions received straightforward answers. "I came to you with my young lady, Peggy," said the housekeeper," because I had need of help and a friend, and I knew I could rely on your friendship for me." Peggy nodded gravely, as she raked together the coals and laid the birds on them. "I must start for home to-day," cantinued Mrs. Pumpton. "I cannot even stop with you over night 1" Peggy looked relieved. Evidently she had been apprehensive of a longer visit. • We'll talk up what you want, Mrs. Pumpton, after breakfast,' she said, with more hospitalit. than she had before exhibited. "In the mean- time you can rest, or be walking about," she added, with a glance at the open door-way. If this was intended as a hint, it was not taken. Giralda leaned back on the pillows of the lounge, a delicious sense of rest creeping over her tired frame, and Mrs. Pumpton fanned herself with her handkerchief, and watched the movements of her hostess with glances of approval. The table was made ready at last. A black carthern pot of fragrant tea, a dish of dainty birds on toast, a pot of jam and one or two other delicacies, made up a repast which excited Mrs. Pumpton's warmest approbation and sur- prise. "You live like a queen here, Peggy," she ob- served, as Giralda took a seat at the table, and gently compelled her to clO the same. "You will eat with us, Peggy ? " asked the maiden, smiling. "No, Miss. I had my breakfast hours age." was tee reply. "It you will kindly excuse me, and let Mrs. Pumpton wait on you, I will go on with my work." She was readily excused, and bustled about, her back to her guests, trusting to their preoccupation, as it seemed, not to notice her movements. The quick eye of the Park housekeeper, however, trained to observe everything, noted that she was spreading a snowy napkin on a tray, and arrang- ing upon it temptingly a breakfast similar to that set before the travellers, with the addition of a bumbler of milk. _ nen. with a auick glance towards the tabla. and

LADIES' LETTER.

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

Sofa; as colour is concerned, brown appears the fashionable wear, and by this is not meant any particular shade, mixtures of brown and blue, brown and green, and brown and claret colour. They make up most fascinatingly with moire or satin—the former for choice-5n4 these are recom- mended most strongly to au. Provided your tweed is a good one, you may always substitute velvet trimmings later on, when the days get short and cold, and a cosy appLarance is de- sirable.

Capes appear more popular at present than jackets; although the latter will bo more worn later on when a cape wrap is not a sufficient pro- tection against the biting winds. A very useful "convertible" ulster shows the cape, which may be worn over any costume, 'f,2..; a Led. Material makes a great deal of diff4ren:le to this class of wrap, and in alpaca, with siik iaeinve, it forms a delightful dust or travelling wrap, which would be useful throughout the autumn.

Among the pretty millinery have inspected recently is a large hat of black velvet, somewhat of the Beefeater shape, trimmed quite simply with a group of beautiful black feathers, and at the back a tiny bow of velvet, the exeellent effect being further intensified by the addition of a pleating of bright cerise-coioured chenille, set some inch or se from the edge of the under part of the brim, In just such touches as these is the skill of the really good milliner shown. Another is a toque of cream-coloured and blackefelt, the crown is black, and the double fluted brim black and cream; amongst the folds of the felt nestle three swallows, a fourth almost hidden under the brim, while two poises of violets fittingly accompany the feathered heralds of spring. Again there was a smart velvet bonnet, with ruche or ostrich round gown, and with ostricke.nd spreay plume.

Bonnets are for the most part rather square and wide in front, and come down low at the back, or, to speak more accurately, the trimming is arranged to fall over the knot of hair. Crowns of gold, silver, or jet openwork lined with coloured velvet are very popular, and the brims in many instances consist of loops of velvet ribbon, with a large square bow and either a buckle and osprey, or a couple of slender wings in front. A pretty bonnet has a loose, puffed crown of dark cerise velvet, and a folded brim of the same covered with jetted net, a cluster of black feather pompons and a tuft of jetted osprey being placed at the side. The strings are of black velvet. This style of bonnet—i.e., with a full velvet crown—looks very well if the brim is formed of a band of fur—petunia velvet, and chinchilla crimson, crimson and sable, and dark green and the new grey fox, which is known as zitka, being among the most satisfactory combinations.

Velveteen will be much worn this winter, and many of the new models in this material are excessively pretty. Claret, cerise, red, purple, blue, green, and tabac brown are the most fashion- able colours. A light powder-blue velveteen is made with a full, short bodice, finished off by a waist-belt with a large bow in front, a folded eollar, with a lace tie beneath of old lace, giving a somewhat quaint cff !ct ; the &ewes are of the gigot shape, and the quite plain skirt just touches the ground.

WHAT IS SPRAT DAY?

The Ninth of November seems says " Little Folks," to have a special interest for Londoners. It is the day on which the new Lord takes up his office, after an extra- ordinary procession, called the Lord Mayor's Show, through some of the chief stre.ets. It is also the birth- day of the present Prince of %Vales, an event that leads to a good deal of feasting and illumination in the West End of the town. But in the eyes of some folk its lead- ing claim to fame rests on the fact that it is the begin- ning of the sprat season, and hence its popular name of Sprat Day.

A BISHOP ON UNDENOMINATIONAL EDUCATION

BIGOTS REPROVED.

The Bishop of Rochester (Dr. Randall Davidson, son- in-law of the late Archbishop Tait) in his visitation charge on Monday said it seemed to him almost incon- ceivable how any Christian man who knew the facts could Bp6ak of the religious teaching at present given kinder the London School Board as " worthless " because it was " undenominational." Those who denounced the teaching as useleQs or untrue betrayed either a strange ignorance of facts or a bigoted determination to ignore them.

Mrs. Pumpton's curiosity, as well as her appetite, was quite satisfied. She moved back from the table as Giralda arose and went to the door, looking idly out upon the wide, sun-lit Downs.

A LIN CO LNSIIIRE MIRACLE.. A_C LE.

ENQUIRY BY THE LOCAL PRESS.

RESULT.

Rumours of a late remarkable occurrence in this dis- trict (says a Lincolnshire newspaper) have led to our • sending a reporter to make full enquiries and ascertain the precise nature of the case. Our reporter accordingly visited Panton, and there found Miss Ann Elizabeth Scott (whose oam3 had been mentionod in the rumour- that reached us) living at home with her parents. She appeared to have recently passed through a severe ill- ness, but the fresh colour peculiar to the country youth of Linc3lushire was returning to her cheeks. Our re porter, having introduced himself, stated that he had ' been deputed to ascertain the truth of the statement that a remarkable cure had been effected in her case, and enquired whether it was true. "Oh yes, it is quite true," said Mrs. Scott with delight, "and you canno- speak too highly of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, which have saved my daughter's life when doctors' mrdicine failed ; she owes her life entirely to them." The daughter's story was exceedingly plain and straightforward; this is what she said : "Jam 19 years of age. I was first taken ill in March,lB9l,and the doctor told me the disease from which I was suffer- ing was watery blood. I was always able to do some work, but I found great difficulty in getting my breath, and I had a painful headache nearly every morning when I awoke, and always felt tired and languid. I had a most skilful doctor, but his medicine did not cure me. All my friends thought I was in a Decline. I gradually sank lower and lower, and I felt at times as if I was going to die. I had no colour in my lips or cheeks. A relation of mine told me that he had been taking Dr. Williams' Pink Pills,and he had been benefited very much by their use. I was at last induced to try them, and in less than a fortnight's time felt that they were doing me good. I improved so much that I continued taking Dr. William's Pink Pills, and now after having taken only eight boxes I feel completely cured." Our reporter found that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills had been purchased by Miss Scott from Mr. E. IHE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 3 1894.

the sets in Golborne-st. near to Walker's Brewery. He said that they were in a terrible state, and one day last week his horse fell down. He had been told that many horses had fallen down at the same spot. Councillor LONGTON was apprehensive that there was going to be a large amount of expenditure in connection with the Committee during next month. They were going to accept a tender (which is quoted aoove)which would entail the expense of £3,400. Alderman WRIGHT: What do you say? Councillor LONGTON : I refer to Mr. Melbourne's tender. _ _ .

Alderman WRIGHT, Why that is only LEW (Laugh- ter). Councillor LONGTON then saw his mistake, and con- tinuing he said that he thought the Committee ought to abolish the system of grids for the sewers in the open streets, and ought to adopt the system of shaft chim- neys. Councillor BENNETT said that he was going to ask the mime question. Alderman WRIGHT had promised to consider a question on the subject which he put at the last meeting. Councillor BEAMAN said that the present system of having grids in the streets was an unscientific system of ventilating, and the fact that the Committee were accepting tenders for 180 iron grids led him to believe that there was going to be no improvement. They had ample facilities for taking away the noxious gases in the intercepting depots, and it could be done at a very nominal expense. Alderman HARRISON said that he remembered the present system being adopted, when Mr. Glazebrook Rylands, one of the most scientific men who ever sat in that Council, advanced it on sanitary grounds, and was supported by the opinion of Sir Wm. Rawlinson. .Of course it was possible that they might be mistaken. Councillor Woocs.—l wish those gentlemen resided ha Church-5.t. They would soon give up their opinion. Councillor BEAMAN said there was a consensus of opinion at the Health Congress that ventilating grids are an unmitigated nuisance. Alderman WRIGHT promised Councillor Davies that his complaint would be attended to at once. He could not give that night a complete answer to Cr4uncillor Ben- nett's questi4u, but he had been informed by the Borough Surveyor that the rainfall had been abnormally light. During the past two months it had only been against 5i during the corresponding period last year. This represented in September 1893, 42,000,000 cubic feet of rain water,against 10,000,000 in September 1894. There had, therefore, been a very small .rainfall, and this had no doubt led to the bad miens. No doubt with a greater rainfall the sewers would be much better flushed. He would promise that the matter of the ven- tilating shafts should receive the committee's attention. The minutes were then passed.

Councillor SMETHURST said he had had complaints made to him that when men had gone to repair pipes they had shut off the water without giving notice to the inhabitants in that particular neighbourhood. In some cases people ha vi been left without more than a pint of water in the house. He thought that orders should be given to the men to give notice in such cases. The minutes were confirmed. WIDENING OF BRIDGE-STREET.

Councillor PERCY Davms moved the following resolu- tion :—" That the resolution of the Council passed at the last meeting relating to the letting of the shop lately occupied by the late R. W. Marsh be rescinded, and that the shop be taken down with,llut any further delay in order to complete the frontage on the westerly side of Bridge-street in accordance with the plan recendly ap- proved by the Street Improvement Committee." Alderman SUTTON asked if Councillor Davies would have any objection to withdraw his resolution. He had fully considered the question that was introduced in that motion since the last Council meeting, and he had come to the conclusion that perhaps there was some reason for re-considering it, and that the complaints of some owners of property in the locality were entitled to further consideration. (Hear, hear.) If Councillor Davies would, therefore, agree to the withdrawal of the resolution he would undertake that it should be brought before the Street Improvement Committee again, and receive due attention. Councillor DAVIES said he was afraid he should hardly be doing his duty to the persons who had asked him to bring the meter before the Council if he allowed it to drop. In 1885 a certain number of buildings were sold by the Corporation, and he had been told by two of the purchasers that they bought the land on the understand- ing that the Corporation were going to improve Bridge- streeb.

Councillor HEPHERD rose to a point of order. He should like to ask the Town Clerk if the question eomplied with the Standing Orders, and if it was in order.

The TOWN CLERK: I have explained it to Mr. Davies. Councillor FIEPHERD : Then it is not in order?

The TowN CLERIC: No. Councillor DAVIES, after being informed that he had not given five full days' notice, produced the following memorials from the inhabitants of Bridge-street :—" We, the undersigned ratepayers, learning from the report of the last Council meeting that it was resolved to re-let the premises lately occupied by R. W. Marsh for a term of three years, respectfully urge you to rescind that resolu- tion, and proceed to carry out the long-delayed improve- ment, by the removal of the premises in question, which have been an obstruction to the traffie for many years, that property having been purchased by the Corporation for this special object. We strongly object to the pro- posed delay, as we consider the reasons for prompt action are obvious to all." "We, the undersigned rate- payers, owners and tenants in Bridge-street, having signed a memorial in favour of rescinding the resolution of the Council respecting the letting of the late R. W. Marsh's premises, wish to supplement that memorial by the addition of one from ourselves, pointing out that the owners purchased the land from the Corporation, and that the tenants became occupiers on the full understanding that the street would be widened to the bridge as soon as the property came into the hands of the Corporation. Under these circumstances, therefore, we feel that no further delay should take place over this matter,and respectfully urge the Council to reconsider their decision, and remove the obstruction which has so long been an eyesore to everybody and in- jurious to trade generally." He consented to withdraw the resolution on the understanding that Alderman Sutton would bring the matter forward at the next com- mittee meeting.

CEMETERY COMMITTEE.

Councillor SKELTON hoped that one recommendation would never be adopted, that of not closing free graves until they were filled up. Alderman HARRISON in reply said that the graves in ether cemeteries were covered over, and there there was nothing objectionable to be seen. The report of the ngistrar was only a recommendation or a letter to the committee.. An increase in the Cemetery staff had been in contemplation wilen Mr. Leigh was alive, and it had nothing to do with the present registrar. The minutes were then passed. CRAWLED ON HER HANDS AND

KNEES.

"I could no longer walk upstairs; I had to crafel up on my hands and knees as best I could." Now isn't that a pitiable picture? Wasn't it a sad and sorry thing for a woman to have to get upstairs like a wounded animal? We should say so. It is enough to make your heart bleed to think of it. Yet it came about simply enough, and might have happened to you or to me as well as to her. She is all right now; she has got over it ; but perhaps you would like to hear what put her in that .condition. There are things, you know, that break no bones and tear no flesh, yet cripple people as badly as blades, bullets, or bludgeons. She tells the story herself. " For the past ten years," she mays, "I never knew what it was to be really strong and well, but I got on fairly up to October,lB9o. Then I began to feel tired and weary. The least exer- tion seemPd like bard work ; it had the effect of hard work. I had a bad taste in the mouth and a poor ap- petite. Whatever I ate appeared to lie on my chest heavy and cold like lead. There was pain and a sense of tightness at nay chest and sides, and a pain in the back that made me stoop. My hands and feet were cold and clammy, and hot sweats and flusnes of heat came over me that made me feel as if I must surely faint away.

"Besides these things there was that curious feeling in the stomach, a kind of knowing, longing sensation which nothing satisfied. As time went by a dry, hack- ing cough set in and I began to spit up a good deal of thick phlegm. Then came the night . sweats, which were soon so bad as to wet my linen ; and when I woke in the night the perspiration would be trickling down my chest. My breathing got so short that I had to fight for breath as though the air had been pumped out of the room.

"By-and-by I was so weak I could no longer walk up- stairs, but had to crawl up on my hands and knees as best I coulsl. Gradually I wasted away. Most ef the day I it in a chair. But for my youn children I should have been put in bed. My husband and all who saw nse thought I had consumption and must die. I could take only liquid nourishment.

" During the course of !my illness I was under different physicians, and took their medicines, but derived no real benefit from them.

"In May of this year (1892) I went on a visit to Burnham Beeches in hope a change of air might do me good. Whilst there, my sister-in-law, Mrs. Puddidfied, told me of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup. She gave me a few dokes out of her bottle, and I was greatly re. lieved, by it. When I return.d home, my husband at once got me a bottle from the stores in White hart- street.

" After taking this medicine for a fortnight, I was better than I had been for years. Soon the cough and night sweats left ms, and I have never been so well as I am now. I tell everyone that Mother Seigel's Syrup has made me .a new creature—as though life had begun afresh with me. I wish others to know it, for th9usands of women are ill, not knowing where to turn for help. Yours truly, (Signed) Mrs. Louisa Newell, Gordon-road, North Town, High Wycombe, Bucks, November 18th, 1892."

A great many people will read Mrs. Newell's pathetic little account of herself, and among them will be women who are in the same plight she was in. They will won- der why the medicine she mentions cured her so quickly in case she had consumption. The explanation is simple. She had no disease of the lungs at all ! she had chromic indigestion and dyspepsia, which imitates and counter- feits consumption so closely as to deceive expert phy- sicians. This it does because itepoieon, created in the stomach, reaches and affects all the other organs. Set the digestion right, and, in nearly all c-ases, you soon set everything right. We say this to encourage those who think there is no help for them. They have been treated for complaints they are not steffering sit h. , Never give up the fight until you ask Mother Seigel to hear a band in it. Most ailments can be cured if you go at them the right way.

NEGLECT OF FAMILY AT GOLBORNE.

STARTLING DISCLOSURES.

At the Newton Sessions on Saturday, Peter and Bridget Travis, residing at 6; Gas-street, Golborne. were summoned for neglecting their six children on October 13th, and thereby causing them unneces- sary suffering.—Mr. J. W. Knowles, solicitor, Widnes, said that he prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C. The facts of the case were that Inspector McAteer visited the house of the defendants on the above date, and found the wan, his wife, and all the children at home. The man was sitting in a corner, drunk, and the Inspector saw a quart of beer taken out of the house, but he could not say whether it belonged to the defend- ant. On examining the house, he found downstairs, an old table and two broken chairs. Upstairs he found some stinking rags and old clothes. The children were covered with body lice and fleas, and their condition was deplorable. The man was in receipt of an income of 255. per week, and a boy of his was also earning money, so that there was an income altogether of 38s. a week. In the house, the only food the Inspector found was an old, hard, loaf.—lnspector McAteer stated that he visited the house in company with P.C. Simpson and found the children in a terrible state of dirt and filth. Sarah, aged 13, was dressed in a torn dirty chemise, and had neither boots nor clogs. Her hair was matted with dirt and filled v.itti vermin and knits. The girl's body had not been washed for a considerable time. William, aged 11 , Peter (9), Annie (8), Mary (8). and Maggie (4), were all in a similar condition. The bed on which the children slept consisted of an old mattress saturated with urine, and old rags for bed-clothes. The bodies of all the children were bitten severely with body lice. He had visited the house previously with P.C. Simpson and Dr. Part, and on that occasion he told the parents that he would overlook the matter if they would look after the children betteribut as they had not,he had instituted the present proceedings.—Corroborative evidence was given by P.C. Simpson, P.C. Blundell, and Dr. Part, after which the Bench decided to send the male prisoner to gaol for one month, and the female prisoner for 14 days. Thomas, Margaret and Anne Johnson, 2, John-street, Golborne, were iummoned for a similar offence com- mitted on October 5, h. Thomas Johnson did not appear and a warrant was issued for his apprehension. —Mr. Knowles said that this was by far a worse case than the first. 'the man Johnson was an industrious, hard working man, and brought his money home to his wife Margaret. The only complaint against him was that he did not see that the washing of his children was property attended to. The young woman, Anne John- son, was the daughter of Thomas and Margaret, was 20 years of age, and had had twe illegitimate children, but they had not bean affiliated. The Inspector would swear that he did not think that she wishes to have the responsibility of keeping them. According to law, the grandparents have an equal liability with the mother of illegitimate children. All the children—two of Margaret's, and two of Anne's—were below the nor- mal weight, and the only cause that the _doctor could attribute it to was neg'ect and starvation. The father brought the money home, and it was there drunk away, and the children were left totally neglected. He asked the magistrates to deal severely in this case.—lnspentor McAteer said that on the fifth of the present month he [ visited the house of the defendants in company with P.C. Simpson,where they found four children. Margaret Johnson, aged 11 rears, was dressed in an old frock and etticoat, and a portion of an old chemise. She wore a [ pair of old boots and steckinas,and her hair was matted. Her body was covered with marks of body lice. Julia, aged 15 years, was in a similar condition, and she had evidently not been [washed for a week or ten days, for her flesh was crusted with dirt. The two illegitimate children—Anne, aged 1 year and 10 months, and Mat- thew, nine months—were dressed in old garments, and their bodies were completely eaten with vermin, and were also mnch emaciated. He was much concerned with these children, as he thought they would die. He brought in Dr. Part, and the two examined the house. In the front kitchen there was simply nothing but an old form with a few old rags placed upon it, to be used as a bed for the child Anne. The children's clothing was covered with excrement, and there was no food in the cupboard. Upstairs the beds were in a filthy con- dition. In the room in which Anne Johnson and her two children slept there was nothing but an old bed of straw, and it resemb!ed an outhouse. Upon the straw was an old mattress seurated with urine. He asked the husband how lie acccuuted for the state of the house, and he replied that he did what he could, it was his wife's fault. The child Anne when weighed only weighed 151hs . whereas the normal weight of a child her age was 231b5., and Matthew weighed 111bs., and should have weighed 151135. He never saw a worse case than the present one since he joined the Society. When he took hold of the gal ments of the children,the vermin dropped to the fl )or.—Dr. Part gave similar evidence. Healso said that when he lifted up a little of the straw in the back room, "the buzz of the vermin was appal- ling." The vermin must have been engendered by the state of the straw. When he told Mrs. Johnson to lift up the clothing of the child Anne, "the vermin and body lice fell in shoals on the floor." There was no doubt that the children suffered from systematic neglect.—The magistrates decided to send each defendant to goal for a month's hard labour.

NEWTON DIVISION BOWLING ASSOCIA- TION.-PRESENTATION OF CUP.

On Saturday evening representatives of the var- ious clubs comprising the Newton Division Bowl- ing Association,met at the Pied Bull hotel, Newton, for the purpose of taking part in the presentation of the Legh challenge cup to the North Ashton Club which having won IG, two years in succession, now become the absolute possessors. The proceedings were initiated by a knife and fork tea, to which, about 25 were present. the catering of Mr. Dorman being all that could be desired. After tea, the cloth was removed and Mr. J. Robinson presided. The Hon. I'. W. Legh should have officiated as chairman, but at the last moment he telegraphed regretting his inability to be present. The Chairman was supported by Messrs. W. Liptrett, J. Crompton, Szc. The loyal toast having been duly honoured. The Chairman gave, " The President, the Hon. T. W. Legh M.P." Re felt sure every member present would drink the toast with enthusiasm. In Mr. ' Legh he thought they had a noble presidenqa gentleman who was always willing to do all he could in connection with their bowling association. (Applause.) After the Secretary had read letters of apology. The Chairman proceeded with the presentation of the cup. It was, he said a very pleasant duty, particularly as the team to have the honour of the presentation was the North Ash- ton howling club. (Bear, hear ) North Ashton at their last gathrring were. the recipisnta of the cup. and this year they had been successful in beating all the clubs, with one exception in the competition, therefore having won the cup twice, they were entitled to it in perpetuity (Applause.) He felt sure he was quite right in ravine that North Ashtmn had persned their course with an enthusiasm and energetic rpirit which was worthy of them. They made up their minds more than a year ago ,to win the cup, and they had been successful, and al- though he was proud to hear that Earlestovvn ' bad been successful in beating them in one match, he thought it rejected credit upon North Ashton in winning the cup t.wo years in succession. (Applause.) He thought he was right in saying that it was never expected that one club would win this cup two years in succession, bilt t hat it would go round the ' clubs, and in same five or six years be won by one of the clubs outright. They w“re now in the position of having to ask their President, or some other gentleman to give another cup for competition. He then handed the cup over t.a the captain of the North Ashton team , (Mr. Welsby) on behalf of the club.—Mr.Welsby briefly acknowledged on behalf of his team.—The Chairrna-ri , gave the toast "Chairman if Committees." Mr.Liptrott, hP said had a wide experience of bowling, and was a most estimable chairman of committees. He referred to the question of matches being played on neutral grounds, which he thought was a very fair proposal, as this would bring the club into close and keen competi- tion.—Mr. Smith intimated that next year he would give a set of bowls and bag to the club which cam,. second in the competition. (Applause.)—Mr. Liptrott responded to the toast, and alluded to the change to be made in respect to clubs playing on neutral grounds, which he thought Would he a beneficial one. (Hear, hear.)—Other toasts followed, and songs were given hv Messrs. J. Finch, Smith, Kent, and others, a pleasant evening being spent.

At Birmingham on Wednesday, two publicans, named John Brinkworth and John Henry Davidson, were each fined £2O for permitting betting on their premises. At Wolverhampton, for simil r offences, Henry Cook aud Esther Hughes were each fined £2l. John Thomas Hall, cashier at Messrs. Leatham, Tew, and Co.'s bank, was senteneed by the Wakefield magis- trates on Wedneslay to six months' hard labour for stealing 1,257 £5 notes, the preperty of his employers.

POACHING ON THE BEWSEY ESTATE.

POACHERS AND POLICEMEN.-A FREE FIGHT.

At the Newton Petty Sessions, on Saturday, James Howson and Thomas Burke were charged on remand with being on land in search of game, at Burtonwood, belonging to Lord Lilford. Mr. A. E. White, Warrington, said that on Tuesday night, the 23rd,about 10 30 p.m., as the gamekeepers were going their rounds, they noticed a gate was open which ought to have been closed. On reaching a field in the occupation of John Warburton. they saw the prisoners and another man on a field occupied by Mr. Faircicugh. On seeing the keeper Lee, they called out, 44Come on, you B-," but when they saw there were watchers with them, the men turned and ran away. They succeeded in overtaking theA prisoners, but the other man who was with them got a*ay. Prisoners bad in their possession twe nets, pegs, and two sacks with live rabbits in them. Poaching on the Bevvsey estite was becoming a nuisance, and every dark night men I went out poaching in a wholesale maltner. He would also point out that the offence of night poaching was a very serious one, and if any game was to be left on this estate something would have to be done: This was corroborated by George Henry Lee and another game- keeper. The Chairman said they considered this one of the voarst poaching cases that had ever been before the Bench,the prisoner Rowson would have to go to gaol for three calendar months with hard labour ,and Burke , would be fined 40a. and costs or in default one month's imprisonment. they would be also bound over after- wards for six months, themselves in a surety of £lO ' and one surryofClO. -

Peter Rowsen, an old man, was also charged with a similar offence, and assaultlng the poiice on October 26th. He pleaded guilty to the first offence and not guihy to the secoAd. Mr. A. E. White also prosecuted in this caee,and said that about 4.30 a.m. that Saturday morning P.C. Barnes in company with two other con- stables, met the prisoner and some more men in New. road, Dallatn. They stepped out in front of them and something like a free fight occurred. The prisoner was overpowered and he was feund to have nets and 29 rabbits in Ms possession.—P.S. Barnes said he met the men in New-road, Dallarn. As soon as they saw hini and the other two constables thee threw stones at them. They secured the prisoner and then went towards the other men. They were again saluted with a shower of stones, and a free fight ensued, which lasted about tea minutes. During the fight he knocked one of the men down but he was carried away by one of his mates who ran over the canal bridge and turned it and so got away. When they met the men they were coming from the direction of Burton Wood and was going towards War- rington. A large stone was now produced which had been found in the prisoner's pocket, and which witness said was similar to the ones thrown at them by the other men. The prisoner tried to strike him (witness). He saw him strike Constable Callaghan.—P.C. Callaghan corroborated, and said that prisoner struck him in the face and kieked him.—Prisoner said he had tried to get work in Warrington, but had failed. Pe went out to try arid catch a. rabbit or two and the first time he had got caught himself. He kept the stone in his pocket for a dog that had flew at him when he had gone up Burton- wood before.—Mr. White stated that prisoner had been up many times before for similar offences. He was fined ES and costs for the poaching affray, and for the assault he was sent to gaol for one moeih. In default of paying the fine one month's imprisonment. The nets, &c., 'I would also be forfeited.

The excess of births over deaths was 33. The death rate from all causes was equal to 15.7 per 1.000, against 16-7 the average of the previous four weeks, and again 25-4 the corresponding, week of last year. The deAlt rate from the seven chief zytuotic diseases was equal to 2-7 per 1,000, against 2 the average rate of thefour- previous weeks, and against 1.5 the corresponding week ot 1893. THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 3, 1894.

Lord Rosebery IN his speech at Bradford, on and the Saturday, Lord ROSE BERY dealt House of Lords. in an adequate fashion with the question of the House of Lords. It is a subject which cannot, in fact, be any longer ignored. Has the House of Commons, which is representa- tive of the nation generally, or the House of Lords, which represents the prejudices and inter- ests of a small and priveleged section of the nation, to have the dominant voice in the govern- ment of the country ? That is, when we come down to the essential facts of the situation, the question which has new come up for definite settlement. Since the present House of Com- mons was elected it has passed several legislative measures dealing with important subjects in accordance with the pledges given to electors. These measures were in the usual course transmitted to the House of Lords for consideration and the House of Lords has in some instances entirely destroyed them, and has eliminated from others some of the provisions which in the opinion of the majo- rity of those who elect the Horse of Commons were most valuable. In other words, the House of Lords,the unrepresentative Chamber, arrogates to itself the right to revise and reject the work of the House of Commons, the representative Cham- ber. What is more,the country has been plainly told that all the other leading measures on the pro- gramme of the present Government will receive the seine treatment. To sit still and tolerate this sort of thing would simply be equivalent to hand- ing the control of the affairs of the country over to the extremest section of Tories in perpetuity No matter how Liberal or how Radical might be the complexion of the House of Commons, the

House of Lords could be relied upon to allow no new law to be promulgated which had not been shaped to the Tory model. The Liberal party has no desire to be placed in any such leading strings, hence the now irresistible demand for such a readjustment of the relations between the two Houses of Parliament as will make the House of Commons supreme. The Government have now, it is evident, determined to respond to the wishes of the party they lead. Lord ROSEBXRY'S speech gives ample evidence that the subject has been closely considered in all its aspects. To "shift the balance of the Constitu- tion" as the Premier points out,is not a simplet matter. This is a time of ußrest and change,but it is no*, revolutionary time. Lord ROSEBERY can- not walk into the Upper Chamber, as Cromwell in his day walked into the House of Commons, and order the peers to clear out with all their para- phernalia. What is done will have to be done by strictly constitutional methods, and it cannot be forgotten that the peers themselves are a part of the Constitution. There seems to be, therefore, a concensus of opinion that, under the circum- stances, the plan of procedure now proposed by Lord ROSEBERY is the best. He proposes "to bring the House of Commons into play." The House of Commons will be asked to proceed, as it has proceeded in the past in its contests with the House of Lords, namely, by resolution. The Government proposes to invite the HOUS3 to pass a resolution declaring that it is the "pre- dominant partner" in all legislation, with the view, of course, of depriving the Lords of their veto. The Lords will, no doubt, under the guidance of the Marquis of SALISBURY contest the right of the Commons to make any such change in the constitution, and in that event the PRIME MINISTER will proceed to what he calls the second act in the great drama. He will appeal to the country for a mandate to revise the constitution. It is almost certain that the mandate will be given. When it is given a method of bringing the Peers into subjection will soon be found.

LORD SALISBURY gets more "Mere Brigands." and more emphatic in his allus- ions to trade unionists. A few mor.ths ago he was generally understood to refer to trade unions as "cruel organisations;" in his speech at Edinburgh this week he describes trades unionists as "brigands." Lord SALISBURY is in the habit of complaining after almost every speech that he makes that he has been misunderstood. In the present instance it is hard to see where any , misunderstanding can creep in. The Trades Union Congress, at its last meeting, passed a resolution by a large majority in favour of the , State ownership of all the means of production, and Lord SALISBURY now declares that "when you hear people say that all the instruments of produc- tion ought to be taken into the hands of the State, you know that you are in the presence of mere brigands." Clearly, therefore, trades unionists are, in the estimation of the Marquis of SALIS- BURY," mere brigands." There can be no mistake this time as to whom this choice epithet was in- tended to describe. The Marquis is really very inconsiderate. The Tory workingman must often be in a state of mental perplexity when trying to find reasons for holding what he calls his "political opinions," but how much worse is his position now when his own leader describes him as a "mere brigand." If the Tory trade unionist can stand this he must be a person of peculiar humility.

A GOOD deal has been said at the The Iron Trade. Iron and Steel Trade Conference held this week in Glasgow about the high wages of English ironworkers as com- pared with the wages of foreigners, and it was even resolved that "accurate knowledge of the foreign competition, and the serious prospects of the iron and steel trades" should he diffused amongst the workmen. There was, however, another question touched upon which has a very important influence on every branch of British industry. It is that of preferential or unduly heavy rates of carriage. What is to be said, for example, of the English lines of steamers which carry Continental iron from Antwerp to India for ten shillings a ton while charging fifteen shillings and ninepence for English iron. According to the gentleman who made these statements, these steamers are actually sent to Antwerp to load and then return to London to take on board the English iron which is charged for at a fifty per cent. higher rate. And the com- panies which own these steamers are heavily subsidised by either the English or the Indian government The British manufacturer is also heavily handicapped by the differences in the rail- way rates. It costs the Birmingham producer twelve shillings aid sixpence to send a quantity of material to Birkenhead which could be sent an equal distance over Belgian railways for four shil- lings. It seems from these facts that manufac- turers should not look too exclusively to the wages of their workmen foe relief.

6111 P aNAL. CLAIM FOR DAMAGES BY MESSRS. FAIRCLO UGH

AND SONS.

An action in Admiralty was heard by his Honour Judge Collier,asserted by ,Captains Nowell,and on Friday last week, at the Liverpool County Court, in which Messrs. James Fairclough and Sons, corn mer- chants, Warrington, owners of the steam barque Pioneer and the barge Pirattt, sued the Manchester Ship Canal Company, owners of the steam tug Agnes Seed, for £167 6s. 11d., damages through a collision which hap- pened on the morning of the 22nd of May last, near Wigg's Works, Runcorn. The defendant company counterclaimed for £5O damazes done to their tug, Agnes Seed. Mr. Collinwood Hope, appeared for the plaintiffs, and Mr. Steel for the defendant company. The plaintiffs' vessel had entered the canal through the Western Mersey lock, a short time previously, having come from the Flarrington Dock, Liverpool, on the night tide of the 21st. The barge Pirate contained a cargo of wheat belonging to the plaintiffs, which was consigned from Liverpool to Warrington. The morn- ing was very foggy when the Agnes Smd was sighted. In passing, the Agnes Seed touched the steam barge, Pioneer, which was towing the Pirate, and col- lided with the latter vessel. The Pirate was found to be admitting water, and was run ashore. The plain- tiffs subsequently found that a considerable portion of the cargo had been damaged by water, and that had to he sold bv auction for what it would fetch, and the 1 "• ! Pirate being severely damaged had to be repaired by Messrs. Clover Clayton and Company, of Birkenhead. The master of the Pit,meer alleged that the Agnes Seed, when first seen was going at the rate of about nine knots an hour on the wrong side of the canal, and that as she approached she sounded no whistle or signal. In cross-examination, lie admitted ignorance of a canal regulation, in regard to the way in which the barge was made fast, to the steam barge, and he denied that toe • Ship Canal pi ople called attention to the improper way the barge was made fast. For the defence it was a.heged that the steam barge kept on the wrong side of the canal, and neglected the necessary precautions for preventing a co!lision. 'lbis-was denied by the master of the Pioneer, who alleged that he observed all the Ship Canal regulations, and als,, th,!., regulations for preventing collisions at sea, Ile denied that he was ever on the port or wrong side of the canal.

After hearing a considerable number of witnesses on both sides, his Honour delivered judgment. He said that after consulting and taking the aovice of his asses- ors, he had arrived at what he feared was a most un- satisfactory decision, which was that neither side had made out its case. The judgment would therefore be a nonsuit on both sides, with no order as to costs. BARGE TRAFFIC ON THE SHIP CANAL.

It is probable that there will be considerable develop- ment of barge traffic on the Ship Canal in the near future. Some private firms are already engaged in the barge traffic, the rates of carriage from Liverpool to Manchester being 33. 6d. and upwards.

FINAL LEAGUE CONTEST.

On Monday night a grand swimming gala and the final in the Liverpool and District League Water Polo Competition was held in the Corporation Baths,Legh-st.,under the rules of N.C.A.S.A. Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P., took the chair. From the first opening of the doors an unending stream of people continued to pour in, until at 7 30 the building was crowdea.

The hon. officials were—Judges : Messrs. J. Grace,Bes- wick and Toft ; Handicappers ; Messrs. Derbyshire and Hunt (Manchester) ; Starter : Mr. J. Grace ; Check Star- ter : Mr. H. Toft ; Time Beepers : Edward Burrows and. J. Bowen ; Hon. Sec., W. S. C. : Mr. T. G. Grounds; Hon. Sec., to Gala Committee : Mr. Stanley Wilson ; for the polo match the Referee was A. Thomson, Esq., Liverpool: Goal Scorers : E. H. Lowdey (Liverpool Crescent), J. Finegan (Liverpool Sefton) ; Timekeeper : F. D. Nevin, Hon. Sec. Liverpool and District Water Polo Association.

HANDICAPS.

The proceedings were commenced with the first heat in the 50 yards barred handicap, the Ist prize for which was a gold medal. The first three heats were fully re- presented, viz., five swimmers in each. The winners were respectively, P. Isherwood, T. S.C. ; R. Tunstall, St. H. S.C. (swim over) ; and W. Creighton, W. S.C. In heat 4 there were four competitors, of whom J. W. Coe, 0. S.C., came in first. Or toe three competitors in heat 5, W. Ashton, W. S.C., was an easy first. Four swimmers competed in the 6th heat,and the winner was H. Greenhalgh, O.W. S.C. The next two heats were both represented by three oompetitors,and the respective winners were—W. Gadd, V. S.C., and E. A. Jones, W. S.C. The full number of men swam in heats 9 and 10, which were very hotly contested, and the winners were—C. Jolliffe, W. S.C., and A. Jones, Everton S.C. In the last two heats there were three competitors, and both winners were of the samo cub, viz., D. W. Bailey and R. Cheadle, Vernon S.C. The first in each heat swam in the final.

TrERS BREAKS A WORLD'S RECORD. The 100 yards open handicap was the next event for which there were 8 heats and 4 prizs, viz :—Lst prize, marble clock, value £3 35.; 2nd priz., gold medal; 3rd prize, gold centre medal; 4th prize, silver medal; and a fast time prize, overcoat, value £2 2s. given by the Grand Ch.,thing Hall Co.. Bridge-st. The first heat was won by W. Burroughs, St. Helens Swimming Club and the 2nd in which Tyers (amateur champion) and Shar- rack. St. Helens, took part,Sharrack going 3secs. before Tyers. A better race could not possibly have been witnessed and when Tyers won and it was announced that he had also beaten the world's record, the applause was tremendous. His time for the 100 yds. was 62 1-5 secs. thus beating the world's record 1 sec. and 2-5. The remaining heats were more scantily represented, the results being as follows :—(3), F. htierwood, T.s.c.; (4), C. Pennington, St. Helens S.C. ; (5), W.G V.S.C. swim over; (6), P. Ripley, St. Helens S.C. ; (7), E. A. Jones. W.S.C. ; (8) J. Treanor, L.G.S.C. The first in each heat of this handicap also swam in the final. Finals: 50 yds.handicap :-Ist, Greenhalgh, O. W.S.C.; 2nd, Ashton, Warrington; 3rd, Jones, Everton : 4th, Gadd, V.S.C. 100 yds. handic..ap :-1, Isherwood, Tyldesley ; 2, Pennington, St. Helens; 3, J. Treanor, L.G.S.C. ; 4, Jones, Warrington. Before the commencement of the polo match Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P., expressed his pleasure at seeing such an excellent exhibition, and congratulated the club in having such an excellent bath. He discoursed on the value of the art of swimming in many instances. The club he thought was very lucky in having such good secretaries as Mr. T. G. Grounds and Mr. S. Wilson. (Applause.)

THE POLO MATCH.

The polo match between Warrington and St. Helens was now commenced, and a great deal of interest was manifested owing to the winning team receiving the Liverpool and District Cup. The teams were as follows —Warrington : Goal, J. F. Dixon; backs, W. D. Sutton, S. Beaman; half-back, Fair Turner (captain) ; forwards, P. Furness, T. Locker, and G. A. Scotson. St. Helens Goal, J. Aspinwall; backs, D. W. Holland, J. Martlew; half-back, R. Sharrack; forwards, A. Marsden (captain), J. Sherwood, and J. M'Kay. At the commencement St. Helens defended the deep end of the bath and Locker fer Warrington was the first to the ball and passed to Fur- ness who dribbled well up. Fouls were at first frequent and from one Locker got the ball and made a shot a goal which was well fisted out. Sharrack imme- diately answered by one of his long shots which Dixon. neatly caught. Even play ensu4d and goal-shots were frequent on both sides. The game grow very exciting when McKay got away and almost scored, Dixon stopping- the ball which Beaman fisted out into the middle again. Turner then got the ball and passed to Locker who with a quick shot scored the first geal of the match amid con- tinued cheering. On the re-start Locker again got the ball and after a neat bout of passing scored again, the ball just getting between the enemy's posts. A moment later Locker passed to Turner who shot at goat, but the ball just tipped the bar and passed over. St. Helens now began to look formidable and getting the ball at the Warrington end, McKay shot the first goal for the visi- tors. From a foul near the posts another chance cropped up but was missed. Turner again shot unsuccessfully and half-time arrived with the score reading., Warring- ton two goals, St. Helens one. The teams now changed ends and the visitors la±gan to give the Warrington goal-keeper trouble; a long shot from one of them only being stopped by the lucky position in which Dixon had his head. The play for a time centred chiefly at the St. Helens end when Sharrock getting pos- session equalizad • the score by another long shot which was quite out of Dixon's reach. Play now became of a keener character neither side gaining much advantage. Sharrack and Locker, Martlew and Scot- son, were playing a regular cat and mouse game. The, home goal was again in imminent danger, two shots passing over the bar in succession. Sharrrck getting clear put in another goal though Locker dig his best to- step him. For repeated fouling Scotson and Martlew were ordered out of the bath though appearances were certainly in favour of the Warrington man. Beaman now got the ball and swam toward the visitors' goal but being too deliberate he was over-reached and the chance was lost. Time was soon called and after a hard fought game the score read—St. Helens three goals War- rington two.

===== 0002601_18941103_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
VARIOUS EXHIBITIONS.

Now followed one of the most interesting features of: the gala viz. : the exceedingly clever ornamental swim ming performance of Master W. H. Cur wen (11) of Birkenhead. He appeared in a sailor suit inclusive of stockings and goloshes :v Inch after diving in he divested himself of. This was followed by an exhibition of the over-arm and side strokes, swimming on the back, pro pelling head and feet foremost, marching on the water,. revolving, motionless floating In various positions, somersaults backward and forward, watrwheel, an ex- cellent imitation of crab swimming, end fi laity the youngster plunged down and brought up his clothes. 'Joey " Nuttall and Professor Barlow (bath-keeper)- now made their appearance. Before the swimmers entered the water it was stated that Nuttall was the un- disputed professional champion of the world. He com- menced his career at the age of 11, and up to now he has. won between 200 and 300 prizes. In his amateur career he beat all opponents,and since he became a professional helms been undefeated: He has won the I,oooyds. so often that'no,switniner will compete with him for it. He Is. also the:possessor of the Sporting Life Championship Cup for a mile his record for which has never been beaten. He now attempted to lower the 250 yards record of' 3 min. 5.4-5 secs, made by J. H. Tyers at the Heywood baths twelve months ago. Professor Barlow swain 4 of the 10 lengths with him in good style. Nuttall kept up- a quick powerful stroke but at the finish it was found that his time was 3 mins. 7 secs. 2-5 or 1 sec. 3-5 below the record.

J. McHugh (amateur plunge champion) gave an ex- hibition long plunge,diving 60 feet (ahnost equalling his record) and remaining 56 secs. under water. He also, gave a short exhibition of swimming. At the conclusion of the gala the Chairman distributed the prizes as the successful competitors filed out.

The following letter from such a prominent source- needs no explanation. The writer is the Rev. 11. Elwyn Thomas, pastor of Tolmers-square Church, London, N.W.. dated June 18th, 1894. "As a rule," he writes, " I put very little faith in the much advertised patent medicines of the day. The more advertised they are, and the more numerous the ' testimonials' published o.nocerning them the weaker is my faith in their efficiency. There are exceptions, however, to every rule. LOCAL NEWS.

BILLIARD HANDICAP AT THE RUNCORN LIBERAL CLUB. A billiard handicap woich has been in progress for nearly two months at the Runcorn Liberal Club for four prizes was concluded on Friday evening of last week. The game resolved itself into five rounds, and finally ended by Messrs. F. Riley,H. Heesom, H. Barnes, and P. Comes respectively winning the four prizes. COLLIER BREAD is the best money can buy.—Advt. INTERESTING LECTURE AT RUNCORN. On Tuesday evenlng, an interesting and instructive lecture was given by Mr.W.H.Chadwick in the Bethesda Congregational School, Runcorn entitled" From Island to Island in the South Seas." There was a fairly large attendance and the lecture, which was much appreciated, was illustrated in various parts by lime- light views. The proceeds were in aid of the London Missionary Society.
