RUNCORN DISTRICT NURSR'S WORK.

_ The "Runcorn Parish Magazine" for this month states that there have been forty-nine cases on the books from V. the 25th September to the 25th October, nineteen of which were fresh ones. The cases are classified as fol- lows :—Chronic 9; medical, 18; surgical, 5; and typhoid, 17. Of these, two have been taken to hospitals in Liverpool. three have died, and 15 are convalescent. Two chronics are receiving occasional visits. This leaves 27 cases on the books. Twenty patients have received occasional visits, of these one has died. There have been 298 visits paid to cases, and 69 occasional visits, making the total number of visits 367. COLLIER'S is. BUTTER is the gem in the market.—Advt. DEATH OF AN OLD PRESBYTERIAN IN RUNCORN. The death of Mrs. Parr, Pennington's-row, Runcorn, was feelingly referred to on Sunday evening by the Rev. J. Pulford Williams, pastor at the St. John's Presbyte- sian new school. The rev. gentleman said that the deceased lady was the oldest member of the St. John's Church, and connected the present generation of wor- shippers with the earliest history of Presbyterianism in Runcorn. Although she had been too feeble of late years to work actively for the church, yet she had taken a deep interest up to the last in all that affected the spiritual progress of the Cause of God in that place. COLLIER'S sell the best quality goods at lowest possible prices.—. Advt. UNPLEASANT ADVENTURE OF SIR JOHN Ricky. A correspondent states that Sir J. Rigby who has been spending a holiday in Montgomeryshire, met with a strange adventure during his stay at Gregynog Hyll, Lord Sudeley's seat. In company with a young lady, he rode out one morning across the moors with the intention of visiting some mountain pools. The roads hereabouts are of 2, treacherous nature and abounding in bogs. Whilst attempting to cross a stretch of the latter the horse sank knee deep in the mud. Being at the time out of sight of a house and far from the high- way, their situation was none of the pleasantest. For- tunately, the son of a shepherd came by at the time,and succeeded in directing them to the road. Sir John and his compauion returned to Greynog, not visiting the ." pools." FUNERAL OF A RUNCORN YOUTH AT WIDNES. The interment of the late Mr. John Owen, 18 years of age, who had lived with his parents at 29, Martley-st., Widnes,took place on Saturday afternoon in the Widnes parish churchyard. The young man, who died on the previous Thursday in Widnes Hospital from typhoid lever, formerly lived in Runcorn with his family. He was a member of the Holy Trinity Church choir,and the -secretary of the Band of Hope connected with this church. He was likewise a member of the Runcorn Tonic Sol Fa Prize Choir, and had associated himself also with the Runcorn Seamen's Mission, Widnes Bible Class, and the Widnes Church Army. He was widely respected, and the attendance of friends and relatives from both sides of the river at his funeral, was very numerous. A large number of beautiful floral tributes were upon the coffin, and the ceremony at the grave- side, which was conducted by the Rev. Mr. Adams, was most affecting. COLLIER'S BLACK BLENDED TEA. 20. per quarter lb.—Advt FIRE AT Rum:m.4. Considerable excitement was caused in the town on Sunday night at about seven o'clock,through the sound- ing of the " fire buzzer." The brigade quickly ansvveaed the call and were directed to the shop of Mrs. Mary Meadowcroft, 18, Church-st., where a few minutes previously a fire had been discovered by some passers- by. The brigade, who were under the command of Superintendent of Wright and Deputy-Superintendent Wright, found upon their arrival that the fire had been checked somewhat by the discoverers. They, however, at once rendered their assistance, and in a very short time the flames were extinguished. The fire had origi- nated underneath the shop window, where a quantity of matches was stored. It was very fortunate indeed that the fire was so soon detected,or probably the result would have been a great deal more serious,as the nature of the stock in the shop was inflammable. Owing to the prompt actiou of the brigade and other persons the damage done is not more than about £5O, and this is insured in the Lancashire Insurance Company. Natu- rally enough, a large crowd of persons collected outside the shop, but order was maintained by Superintendent Atherton, and several constables. STEALING AND RECEIVING AT RUNCORN. On Wednesday morning at the Runcorn Occasional Police Court before Messrs. F. J. Norman and S. Taylor,

Wm. Henry Hardley,Cooper-street, was charged in three instances with stealing boots one pair from Messrs. Tyler's shop in Bridge-st., another pair from Thomas Whinyates' Church.st., and the other pair from William Craddock, Church-st. Margaret Hardley, mother of tile prisoner, was charged with rece.ving two pairs from her son well knowing them to be stolen.—Evidence was given to show now the thefts had been committed. by

the lad, and further that the female prisoner had Pledged one pair at the pawnshop of Mr. Lightfoot, but was caught by the police whilst trying to pledge another air at Mr. Smith's, Alcock-street.---The boy admitted having stolen the articles, but the mother pleaded that she believed, when she received them from her son, that he had obtained them honestly.—The magistrates, how- ever, were not disposed to believe such a statement and sentenced her to two months' imprisonment, and ordered the boy to receive twelve strokes with the birch-rod. COLLIER'S FRESH IRISH DAIRY BUTTER, 10d. per lb. is ,excellent, —Advt. ALLEGED UNLAWFUL WOUNDING AT RUNCORN. At the Runcorn Police Court on Tuesday afternoon before Mr. F. J. Norman, a boy named Harold Curzon, 23 years of age, who had been apprehended under a Warrant was charged with having unlawfully inflicted grevious bodily harm upon Elizabeth Lyon, eight years of age,on the 20th October.—lnspector Hicks stated that the girl was in a very critical state through the effects of the offence of which the prisoner was accused. He was therefore unable to go on at once with the case, and -asked for the boy to be remanded until (this) Friday morning. The adjournment was granted, the prisoner being admitted to bail in one surety of £25. RUNCORN BRANCH OF WESLEYAN FOREIGN MISSIONS. An advertisement in another column announces the 'fact that on the 11th inst. two sermons will be preached in the St. Paul's Wesleyan Chapel, Runcoin, in aid of the Wesleyan Foreign Missions. The preacher on both occasions is to be the Rev. George Patterson, late of Madras, from whom may be confidently expected two very able sermons. These will in all probability relate to the rev. gentleman's past experiences as a missionary in India, which should indeed prove intensely interest- On the following Monday evening the annual meet- ing in connection with the local branch of the mission Will be held, Mr. S. ACTION BY A RUNCORN QUARRY OWNER.

At the Runcorn County Court before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes on Tuesday afternoon, an action was heard in which Alfred Hunt, stone mason and quarry owner, Runcorn, sued Messrs. Gustavous Bros., Carmarthen-road, Swansea, builders and contractors, for the sum of ..CB, price of stones supplied. The defendant alleged that the stones were u,eless and brought a minter-claim against plaintiff for £ll 6s. 4d., being £3 3s. for loss of time in having to provide other stone, and the rest was for carriage of Hunt's stone and its storage at Swansea, which defendant expected he would have to pay the Railway Company. Mr. Grier, barrister, of Liverpool, instructed by Mr. Burton, solicitor, of Runcorn. represented the plaintiffs, whilst Mr. George Gustavous conducted the case for the defendants.

It transpired that the defending firm gave Mr. Hunt an order for the stone on the recommendation of a Mr. David, who acted as clerk of the works at Swansea- The order was that the stone should be "good, sound, Run- corn red sandstone for building purposes." The stone, which was duly despatched at the beginning of the pre- sent year, arrived at Swansea safely. One portion of it was then taken to the defendant's yard, and was found to be full of sand holes and clay holes, according to the statement of Mr. George Gustavous. The defendants alleged that the stone was of no uso whatever for the purpose for which it was intended ; and Mr. Davicl,who recommended them to order it from the plaintiff, was also of that opinion and condemned it.

• The plaintiff was called and stated that the stone was good and sound, but admittted that when he called upon the defendants in Swansea about it he offered to let them have the lot for 30.3. less if it was taken off his hands, as it would inconvenience him very much if the plaintiff would not have it. The plaintiff, however, de- clined to have the stone.—Joseph Prevor, of Shaw-st., Runcorn, quarryman of 16 years experience, and Thomas Walton, Weston, quarryman, and foreman to the plaintiff, both testified to the stone being good and sound. The last named witness, in cross-exa- mination said that the best Runcorn sand-stone was somewhat of a darker red than the piece produced,which was a sample of that supplied to the defendants. Evidence was then called on behalf of the defence,— William David, architect, and clerk of the works (Tem- perance Hall) Swansea, said he inspected the stone at the railway siding, but could not pass it for the work then in hand.—ln cross-examination he denied that he had entertained any ill-feeling against Hunt, and that that was the reason why he refused to pass the stone.—Counsel then read a letter in which it was stated that witness would remember David Hunt on the next job. This, Mr. Grier contended, meant that David woulct purposely condemn the stone. —William Price, foreman for Messrs. Ormand and Sons, said the stone produced was of a fair quality. It should have been better, however.—George Gustavous, contrac- tor, of the firm of Gustavous Bros., Swansea, spoke to ordering the stone upon the recommendation of Mr. David. He continued taking the stone until it became of an inferior quality, and then stopped his orders. He then wrote informing Mr. Hunt of the matter. The Judge said he had come to the conclusion that the understanding between the parties was that good sand stone should be sent. He therefore gave judgment for defendant with costs, on the counter-claim he gave judgment for Hunt without costs.

FATAL RESULT OF A FALL AT RUN-

CORN.

Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner, held an inquiry on Thurs- day afternoon at the Lion Hotel, Greenway-road, Run- corn, respecting the death of Joseph Price, 75 years of ace, who died on Tuesday night at 7 Ivy-road, from the effects of a fall over the cemetery wall on the 20th Octo- ber.—Dr. MacLennan, said that he was called to num- ber 7 Ivy-road on the 20th October and found the deceased suffering from severe shock, one of his front teeth having been knocked out. There was also an abrasion on the left hand. No futher injuries could be seen. He complained to witness of severe pain, and said than when he was wblking out at the back of the above named house on the 20th Oct. he was tripped up by some stonej coping and fell over a wall into the cemetery grounds. Witness added that the man died on the 30th October, death being due to in- flammation of the brain preceded by concussion and shock to the system caused by the fall.— William A. Ferguson, 7, Ivy-road, son-in-law of the deceased deposed to hearing his father groan at the back ol the premises at about seven o'clocck on the date mentioned. On going nearer witness heard his father say to himself that he was hurt. Witness locked over the Cemetery wall and saw deceased standing against it at the bottom. He then procured a ladder, went down, and fetched him up to the top and after taking him into the house, he sent for a medical men. The wall at the back of .the house was only eleven inches high in one portion and was at the edge of a passage which was but three feet nine inches wide. There was no fence be- tween the path and the wall. The deceased had told witness that he had tripped and fallen over the wall into the Cemetery. The night of the 20th October was rather a dark one. The? had only been in the house three days when the accident happened.—Mary Ellen Ferguson, wife of the last witness, also gave evidence.— The Coroner, after summarising the evidence said he thought there could be no doubt that the man had met with his death through accidentally falling over the wall. He fhought that a recommendation from the jury that the wall should be fenced would be a wise one.— To this the jury agreed.—A verdict of " Accidental death" was returned.

THE DISTRICT COUNCILS WARD MEETINGS.

A meeting of the electors of Newton ward was held at the Town Hall, Newton on Friday evening, for the purpose of deciding upon three candidates to represent the ward on the District Council.—Mr. R. Barton presided, but there was a very poor attendance.—The Chairman said the Committee had met to report to the ratepayers, as to the selection they had made of candidates for the ward. After a good deal of trouble, and after approaching several gentlemen, most of whom declined, they had finally hit upon Messrs. R. Stone, F. J. \I ood and J. Simpson, and he would like someone to propose that these three gentle- men be accepted.—Mr. Stuart said he did not quite agree with the way the chairman had put it, as he did not like it to go forth that the Committee were unani- mous. He intended putting up as a candidate, and he would say that the Committee were not unanimous.— Mr. J. Sankey then proposed, and Mr. Williamson seconded that the candidates be accepted and this was carried.—The Chairman said the candidates were not prepared to address the meeting that night, but on some other occasion they would lay their views before them, and answer any question which might be asked them.— The candiaates then thanked the meeting, for accepting them, and said they would do the best they could for their interests. CROW LANE WARD. A meeting of the electors of Crow Lane Ward was held in the Town Hall, on Tuesday evening. Mr. Thos. Ormand presided, and he was supported on the platform by Messrs. C. B. F. Borron, P. Thompson, W. Mills, W. D4vies, T. Unsworth, S. Schofield, Revs. E. W. W. Kaye, and E. R. Davies, &c.—The Chairman, after an- nouncing the object of the meeting, recapitulated the proceedings of the Committee in the selection of can- didates to represent the ward on the district Council. Ten names had been .tiggested, but Dr. Patterson and himself having told the Committee that they had decided to stand for other wards, the number was re- duced to 8. The Committee then determined that about five or six names ought to be submitted to the electors for choice. Six names were chosen but Mr. Thomas Forster, one of them, decided to stand for Viaduct Ward, and this reduced it to five, and these would be submitted to the meeting that night. He then read a telegram which he had received from Mr. Molyneux, who was away in Cardiganshire, as follows. " With- draw my name, writing." So that they had only four names before them.—Mr. Smith also wrote as follows: —" As business of the utmost importance will prevent my presence at the meeting of candidates of Crow Lane Ward this evening, I have reduced my answers to writ- ing to the 10 questions submitted and regret my in- ability to be present."—The views of the selected candidates were then laid before the meeting, and a poll was afterwards taken, voting being—Borron 51, Smith 49, Mills 44, Thompsc4n 15. The first three will there- fore go to the poll with the support of the Committee. A vote of thanks to the Chairman terminated the proceedings. VIADUCT WARD.

_ A largely attended meeting of the electors of Viaduct Ward was held in the Town Hall, EArlestown, on Tues- day evening, to select candidates for the representation of the ward on the District Council. Mr. S. Harvey was called up.m to preside, and he was supported on the platform by Dr. Valentine, Dr. Mounsey, Messrs. J. W. Emmett, T. Ormand, W. Gilmour, J. Mullins, &c.—The Chairman said they were all aware of the manner in which that meeting had been brought about. He said the names of the gentlemen submitted to the committee as candidates for the ward were Mr. F. Harwood, Mr. Emmett, Mr. Ormand, Mr, MacMahon, and Dr. Valen- tine. Mr. Harwood declined to stand. Mr. Emmett, Mr. Ormand, and Dr. Valentine were willing to stand. Mr.MacMahon was somewhat doubtful,and at that time gave them to understand that he would only come for- ward if a third man was wanted. The committee re- solved to put forward at that meeting the names of Mr. Emmett, Mr. Ormand, and Dr. Valentine, thinking those were the only three who would come forward. Mr.MacMahon it appeared had had further pressure put upon him,and was now desirous of havinc, his name put forward. The committee thought that was the best point at which to ask if there were any further nomina- tions. Mr. MacMahon was then nominated by Mr. Duncalfe, and seconded by Mr. Nevitt, and his name was added to the list.—The candidates then addressed the meeting, and a vote when tiken resulted as follows: —Ormand 102, Emmett 95, Valentine 74, MacMahon 60. The first three will, therefore, go to the poll with the support of the committee. A vote of thanks having been accorded the Chairman on the motion of Mr. Emmett, seconded by Mr. Ormand, the meeting dis- persed. WARGRAVE WARD.

On Wednesday night a meeting was held in Wargrave Schools in support of the candidature of Messrs. Coiling- wood, Slee and Thompson for Wargrave ward. Mr. C. B.F. Borron presided, and he was supported on the plat- form by Messrs. Purnell, Owbridge, Catterall, Wright, Haselden and the candidates.—The Chairman congratu- lated them upon having arrived of the position of inde- pendence, of using their votes so that they would be told in the requirements of the district. Electoral wards were now an accomplished fact, brought about entirely by the ratepayers themselves. They should have been in the position they were now in, five years ago. that of having electoral wards. —Mr. Ceteralt said they were threatened with the sternest opposition. He liked opposition,but he thought when the opposition took upon themselves to bring gentlemen to that ward, they might have brought gentlemen who were residents in the ward. It was not creditable to them to think that they could nc,t find 'men in the ward to represent them. (Hear, hear.) As regarded a workingman candidate, what could they call Mr. Slee, but a typical workingman, in the proper sense of the word, and he considered it an insult to bring men from another ward to represent them. He then proposed that the three gentlemen be their representatives . (Applause).—Mr. Owbridge seconded. The candidates were, he said, more practical than any other three candidates selected for any other ward, that he had heard named yet, and he defied any man to say that they were brought forward for party purposes. They were brought forward in the interests of the mest rising ward in the division.—(Applause.)— Mr. James Wright supported the motion, because he thought they could not be better represented than by the three gentlemen on the platform. Wargrave ward was a ward of engineers, an 1 therefore they wanted engineers to represent them,and they had three engineers before them that night, who had pledged themselves to work for the interests of the ward and for the general good of the ratepayers.—(Hear, hear. )—Mr. Cellingwood said he had now represented them for nearly two years on the Board of Commissioners, it was his good fortune to be appointed almost as soon as he came into the place. He felt very soon how inadequately that end of the township was represented on the Board, and he hailed with delight the proposal of dividing the township into wards. He supported that as far as he could, but was sorry to say that it did not meet with success, however when the petition came round he was one of the first to sign it.—Mr. Slee also thanked them for inviting him to come forward, and would serve the ratepayers faithfully. He was not afraid of taking anything in hand and carrying it out if he was elected, whether it he sewage or water questions. (Applause.)—Mr. W. Thompson was a sew candidate and had no artcount to render of past stewardship, but if they did him the honour of electing him one of their three representatives, he would allow no trifling matter to interfere with his regular attendance, and he would in every way strive by thought and study how best to further the interest of each one in the ward. (Applause.)—The motia'n was then put and carried without dissent.—A vote of thanks to the chairman terminated the preceedings.

DEATH OF DR. APNICOLL, J.P., ST.

===== 0002601_18941103_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
HELENS.

Dr. Robert APNicoll, J.P., for twent3-one years medical officer of the borough of St. Helens, died at 12.30 on Tuesday, at his residence, Hardshaw-st., St. Helens. The late Dr. KNicoll was the son of a Wesleyan minister, the Rev. David M`Nicon, of Dundee, who died in Liverpool in 1830. Dr. I\UNicoll was born in Newcastle in 1820, and received his early education at Woodhouse Grove, near Leeds. At fifteen he was put to business, but at twenty was articled to his brother David, Who practised in Liver- pool. In IMS he went to Dublin for his first course of lectures; the second course he took at the Royal Infir- mary, Liverpool ; and the third at St. Thomas's Hospital, Westminster. In April, 1848, he passed his final at the Royal College of Surgeons, and the same year settled down in St. Helens. His first swgery was in New Market-square, where he remained for seven years, and then he moved into Hardshaw- street. In 1850 Dr. M'Nicoll was married to the only daughter of the late Mr. James Glover, of Newton-le-Willows, the issue of the union being one son and four daughters. Mrs. M‘Nicoll, the son, and the eldest daughter have died, and the three surviving daughters are all married. In movements for the public welfare of St. Helens Dr. M‘Nicoll was a moving spirit. In 1860, the year after the formation of the 47th Lanca- shire Rifle Volunteers (now the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment) he joined the battalion,and on his retirement two or three years since, he was granted the honorary title of surgeon-major. He was one of the promoters of the old Mechanics' Institution,which did excellent work in its day. In 1858 was elected a member of the Board of Commissioners for Eccleston,and remained a member until the incorporation of the borough in 1868. He was then returned to the Council for Eccles'on Ward, and was one of the first aldermen. Four years later he re- signed, and becoming a candidate for the new appoint- ment of medical officer for the borough, he was elected. On completing twenty-one years in that. important posi- tion he in June last retired upon a pension of two-thirds his salary. The late doctor was a member of the Free Library Committee, a Wesleyan local preacher, and president of the St. Helens Medico-Ethical Society. Politically he was a Giadstonian,and was made a boriugil rnaeistrate soon after the present Liberal Administnition came into office. The doctor's death was due to acute congestion of ale kidneys, which caused cardiac failure, and he passed away in the presence of his daughters— Mrs. Handyside, Mrs. Bira!r, and ikrr:,.l3urt,ie--ar.d nurse.

LIVERPOOL FARM SCHOOL REFORMA- TORY, NEWTON-LE-WILLOWS. HON. T. W. LEGH M.P.. ON REFORMATORY EDUCATION.

Prizes were presented on Tuesday afternoon to the boys of the Liverpool Farm School at Newton- le-Willows by the Hon. T. W. Legh, M.P. A num- ber of visitors attended and witnessed with inter- est the drill performed by the boys, afterwards in- specting the various departments of the institution. At the prize distribution Mr. E. W. Cropper presided, and there were present, amongst others, the Hon, T. W. Legh, M.P., Mr. A. J. Dalzell, Mr. W. Blease, the Rev. T. R. Mathew, the Rev. F. W. Johnstone, Mr. Robert Stone, Captain Lowe, &c. Songs and Instrumental selections having been contributed by the boys Mr. J. DIGROCED (superintendent) submitted a report upon the work of the school during the past year. He stated that the health of the bo7s had been wonderfully good, and they were bright, cheerful, contented, and well-behaved. Twelve of the boys who had been trained at Colonel M'Corquodale's printing works at Newton, had left during the year, nine of them having obtained employ- ment as printers in Liverpool. Four boys had emigrated to Canada, and were doing well. The great coal dispute with its consequent disorganisation of trade brought a number of old boys to the school seeking assistance. The superintendent, however, was not aware of a single old boy, of whom there were a number in that district, getting into trouble during this crisis. Thanks to the kindness of Lord Newton and others, the boys had been able to enjoy an outing to Southport during the sum- mer. The cost of maintenance and instruction had been £lB Us. sd. per head on the average of 126 boys ; the cost of outfits, £3 O. Bd. per head on 44 boys ; and'the industrial profits, £4.7 is. sd. The Hon. T. W. Legh then distributed the prizes, and delivered a brief address to the boys. He said that no one who took the trouble to read the reports submitted could help being struck with one thing—the difference in the tone of these institutions. Nowadays one scarce- ly saw any allusion to penal reformatories or incurably vicious boys; on the contrary, the reports showed a cheerful spirit cf what he might almost call optimism. which was reflected in the appearance of the place and of its inmates. No one looking at the general brightness of the place would imagine that the boys were kept there against their will. In former days people seemed to think it the right thing to make life as disagreeable as possible for the boys; school life was made as detest- able as possible, and reformatories were really prisons which people erroneously supposed them now to be. He was happy to say that was changed. Every boy was practically enabled to learn the trade for which he was best fitted, and on leaving the school had every opportunity of becoming a useful member of society. The institution had proved successful in bene- fiting the best majority of the boys who had been brought up there, the percentage of failures being ex- tremely small. He was not at all sure that their record of success was not greater than that of boys who were educated at great public schools, such as Harrow or Eaton, and afterwards sent to one of the universities. (Hear, hear, and applause.) A vote of thanks to Mr. Legh concluded the proceed- ings.

THE 'BUS COLLISION NEAR ST. HELENS.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes the ad- journed case of Massey and Connor was heard. It will be remembered that Mr. Massey, tripe dealer, Wilderspeol-road, Warrington, claimed a 6s. from Mr. Edward Connor, Sankey-street, Warrington, as damages alleged to have been caused to the plaintiff's trap by the defendant's 'bus having coliicied with it at Peasley Cross near St. Helens, on June 23. In the absence through illness of Mr. Browne, solicitor, 7,1 r. Longton appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. P. DAvies again represented the defendant.—Dovid Farrell, co o: smith, 146, Cambrian-place,spoke as to the damage di. le to Mr. Massey's trap.—Oscar George Dell, son of mr. Dell, painter, Sankey-street, spoke to the driver of ihe 'bus having been perfectly sober on the night in questi ,TI. Mr. Massey's horse backed the trap into the front part of the 'bus. No accident would have have happened if it had not been for the backing of Massey's horse. The lamps on the 'bus were lit, but there were none lit on the trap. John Hatton, Latchford, 'bus conductor in the employ of Mr. Connor, stated that the driver was sober when lie came home on the night in question.—Walter John Langdon, conductor of the 'bus, stated that while in St. Helens the driver had two glasses of beer with him. The driver afterwards went away saying he was going for his tea. Witness also corroborated Mr. Dell's statement. —His Honour, in summing up, said be was very sorry Mr. Browne was unable to conduct his case. He had unfortunately taken ill since the adjournment of the case, but he was glad to hear that Mr, Browne was much better and he hoped that in a short time he would have the pleasure of seeing him again in court. Mr. Longfton had been asked by Mr. Browne to conduct the remainder of the case, and of course Mr. Longton had been placed at a disadvantage because he bad not the opportunity of hearing the previous witnesses. No fault, however, could be found in the way he had con- ducted the case. The trap in question according to the evidence was visible twenty yards off, and there was a clear road of eight yards for the defendant's 'bus to pass. It was the driver's duty to have kept clear of the trap. He, therefore, attributed the accident to the driver's negligence. He also referred to the unsatisfac- tory way in which the driver gave his evidence regard- ing the quantity of drink hehad on the day in question. He stated that he never had a drink at all, whereas the evidence showed that he had. He gave judgment for £4 17s. 6d. damages for the plaintiff.

THE SHIP CANAL AND THE RIVER NAVIGATION.

TEST CASE AMICABLY SETTLED.

In the Chancery Division on Thursday morning before Mr". Justice Kekewich, the part-heard action, Fairclough V. the Manchester Ship Canal Company was to have been heard. It will be remembered that the case raised a point as to the depth of water in the part of the Mersey at Warrington which is now included in the Manchester Ship Canal, the plaintiffs who are large merchants in Warrington, complaining that sufficient depth was not provided at their quays, as was provided by the com- pany's Act. After a long consultation between the counsel and solicitors engaged in the case Mr. Fletcher Moulton. Q.C., on behalf of the Ship Canal Company,in- . formed his Lordship that they should not have to trouble him with the case. It was a complicated action. As had been hoped tke parties had agreed that the action should be settled on terms agreed to, but which need not be read. His Lordship agreed to an order being drawn up,and congratulated the parties on having mile to the conclusion, though why it had not been done before he could not say.

NOTES FROM LIVERPOOL AND DISTRICT. (BY A LIMB OF THE LAW.)

The Wallasey Local Board who govern the di tricts of New Brighton, Liscard, Egremont, Seacombe and Wallasey, are determined that they will apply for a Charter so that Wallasey shall become a Corporation. A majority of the rate- payers have declared against them, both by a show of hands at a large public meeting which has been held and also by a poll which has been taken on the subject. The Local Board will cease to exist in November and will either become a District Council or a Corporation. The general wish of the rate- payers is for the former, but the present members of the Local Board evidently consider the rate- payers don't know. what is best for them and are going to make the attempt of forcing matters. The probability is, however, that they will not succeed as there is an organised opposition.

At a meeting in Liscard last week there was an interesting discussion on that most perplexing of subjects "Woman." What made it more perplexing to some of the speakers was that their wives were present and they seemed afraid to speak out. One spniker said he thought woman was quite equal to man and she should become a barrister or a doctor. Another said he thought she was more of a " help- meet "than an "equal." Another very naively said he thought they were wasting their time, as whatever they said women would have their own way. However the general opinion of the meeting was that" Women should have fall scope to com- pete with men in all branches." In Liverpool a good many young ladies are now employed in offices as clerks and typewriters and they appear to be all the brighter for their labors, as well as richer.

The fares of the Mersey tunnel have been re- duced. If people have time they prefer to use the ferry boats between Liverpool and Birkenhead, rather than use the tunnel. It is so offensive with foul air that some passengers declare they won't go in it again. The Birkenhead ferry receipts have certainly increased this 'mit twelve months, prov- ing that passengers are quite prepared to lose a little time so that they may get fresh air.

RUNCORN IMPROVEMENT COMMISSIONERS.

Mr. Samuel Taylor presided over the monthly meeting of the above Board on Thursday morning in the Town Hall, Runcorn. There were also present Messrs. Philip,Speakman C.C., William Handley, H. J. Grice, D. Bisbrown, W. Leach, E. Imison,William Hankinson, J. Pritchard, Walter Collier, R. Abel, J. W. Lightburn, T. Lea, J. Meadowcroft, F. A. Lake, law clerk; E. Marshall, secretary; and M. Barker, surveyor.

===== 0002601_18941103_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. McDougall reported that during the past mcnth 31 deaths had been registered, three of which were the subjects of coroner's inquiries. The 28 deaths showed a death rate of 16 8, but incluning the three inquest cases, the rate was 18.6 per thousand of the estimated population. In the corresponding month of last year 39 deaths had been registered.—The report was adopted. Mr. Handley remarking that he thought it very satis- factory.

===== 0002601_18941103_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGHWAYS AND BUILDINGS COMMITTEE.

The recommendations of this Committee were "that curbs and channels be laid down in Windmill-st. as far as the property of the Runcorn Co-operative Society ex- tends in that street ; that the footway on the east side of Station-road be flagged; that the tender of Messrs W. Rose and Co. ,of Manchester, for the supply of 100 yards of hose at is. 2d. per yard, and of five pairs of screw couplings at 93. per pair, be accepted; that the offer of Mr. W. Wallace Copland to give up a strip of land in Halton-road required for the widening of the road con- taining about 42 yards on the following terms, be accepted, viz :—The Board to pay the sum of ten guineas for such land ; to re-erect the wall,coping,piers, gate and railing on the proposed line, to be re-painted with three coats in oil; to flag the footway on the road- side of the fence. It is further recommended that the works as recited be executed." That plans of certain improvements to back premises belonging to Mr. John Poole in Cavendish-st., and to Mr. Arthur Hough, in Church-st., be approved.—The Chairman proposed, Mr. Bisbrown seconded, and the minutes were passed.

THE MARKETS COMMITTEE.

The Markets Committee recommended that the tender of Mr. F. Blyth to supply unitorm clothing for the market superintendent for the sum of four guineas be accepted, Mr. Hankinson and Mr. Lee seconded the adoption of the minutes which were carried.

FINANCE COMMITTEE. This committee recommended that the salary of Mr. Walter Jones be fixed at the rate of £l2O a year, to date from Ist November, 1894 ; and that £7O of such salary be charged on the general fund and £5O on the water fund. That the bills of costs of Messrs. Baker, Lees and Postlethwaite and the law clerk in connection with the Manchester Ship Canal Bill, 1894, be taxed. The committee is of opinion that there is no need to be repre- sented on taxation. Mr.Handley moved the acceptance of the minutes and reports.—Mr. Hankinson seconded.— In answer to Mr. Walter Collier, the Chairman stated that, with reference tc the proposal to increase Mr. Jones' salary, the reason why the salary was paid cut of the two funds was that Mr. Jones gave a portion of his time to another part of the establishment. His time was not wholly taken up by the water office, and that was the reason why the two funds bore the salary.— Mr. Bisbrown objected to increasing Mr. Jones's salary on the grounds that the Board was almost at the end of their tether, and that they were all passing through a season of depression. The time he thought, was very inopportune to make advances in salaries. It might be advisable for adr.Jones to postpone his application until the formation of the District Council.—Mr. Speakman said that the Board, six months ago, asked Mr. Jones to defer his application for an advance of salary and pro- mised that they would grant it. The amount of the advance they did not then state, of course, and the Board would only be fulfilling that promise if they now accepted the recommendation of the committee. The amount was not excessive for a man at the age of Mr. Jones, and possessing the same capabilities.—Mr. Meadowcroft after remarking that Mr. Jones was deserv- ing of the advance on account of the able way in which he had discharged his duties, said that the water works had come out for the past twelve months in a very satisfactory condition. He had intended to make a statement of the financial position of the water works, but had subsequently decided to postpone it for the next meeting. When that time came, however, he was quite sure that the Board would be more than satisfied at the result of the committee's operations during the past twelve months.—The Chairman agreed with the remarks of Mr. Meadowcroft and the other members with regard to the worth of Mr. Jones, and added that he thought Mr. MeaCowcroft might make a statement of the water works condition for the information of the ratepayers.—Mr. Collier thoroughly agreed with the advance, as also did other members. —Being pressed to make a statement with regard to the condition of the Waterworks Mr. Meadowcroft said he had not the in- formation at hand for anything but a short one. He I could, however, say brea-dly that in addition to meeting all expenses they had put aside over £5OO. (Hear, hear.) It must not be forgotten that they had paid £lOO more for coal on acco.unt of the coal strike, and were still paying more than the standard price was before the strike. But notwithstanding all such draw- backs they had still a small balance. (Hear, hear.)— The Chairman thought that the ratepayers ought to feel very gratified at the result of the year-'s working,.—The minutes were subsequently passed, Messrs. Bisbrown and Lea being the only objectors. THE LOCAL GOVERNMENT ACT, 1894.

The Chairman announced that an Order of the Local Government Board had been received prescribing rules for the election of Urißan District Councillors for the present year.—lt was suggested that a public meeting should be called for the purpose of explaining the work- ing of the Local Government Act, 1894,f0r the benefit of the electors. Nothing of a deftite nature, however, was decided.

CONGRATULATING SIR JOHN RIGBY.

The Board unanimously decided on the proposition of Mr. Speakman, seconded by the Chairman to forward the following resolution of congratulation to Sir John Rigby :—We,the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners, in meeting assembled take the opportunity of congratu- lating you on the high distinction that has been con- ferred up.n you as a Lord Justice of Appeal, and we, as your neighbours for so many years, express the great pleasure it has given us that one of our number has risen to such distinction. We trust that your life may he spared to enjoy and fulfil the public duties in the future as you have done in the past. given under our Common Seal, Mr. Samuel Taylor, chairman; Mr. E. Marshall, secretary.—Sir John Rigby.

THE COMMISSIONERS AND THE COMING ELECTIONS.

Mr. E. Imison drew attention to a paragraph which had appeared in the local press having reference to the selection of candidates for the coming election of the District Council. He should like to know if the reports could be described as accurate. It appeared to him to have been a foolish policy on the part of those seeking election on the Council to meet and select for themselves the wards they wished to. represent. The ratepayers felt that it was tor them to decide and pick for them- selves those whom they wished to represent them rather than that the retiring Commissioners should do it for them. He personally, discountenanced such a proceed- ing.—Mr. Lightburn said he had not heard of any such meeting being convened as also did Mr. Grice and others. —The Chairman at length explained that the meeting was very informal indeed. The matter was simply spoken about among those who were present, and some said that they were prepared to contest such and such a ward and other members made similar declarations. But it was more the nature of a conversation than any- thing else, and he thought that more had been made out of it than was necessary.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The usual fortnightly meeting of this Board was held in the Board room of the Workhouse on Wed- nesday morning. Mr. W. Houghton presided, and there were also present Rev. G. F. Roberts, Dr. Patterson, Messrs. W.Gill,E. Auckland, W. Balharry, J. Clarke, Wm. Fairclough, Davies, Pilling, H. Hib- bert, J. Hill, J. Turton, W. J. Shaw, Thos. Smith, J. Ilatton, A. Jolley, A. J. Jolley, John Kilshaw, H. J Longtou, T. Mather, R. Owen, junr., G. Ratcliffe, J C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), C. H. Ward (workhouse master), and H.M. Mines (super- intendent of Palgate Schools).

A NURSE- LEAVES V., ITEIOUT NOTICE.

It was stated in the House Committee's minutes that Nurse Lobley had refused to perform night work and take her turn with the other nurses. The committee recommended that if she persisted in her refusal she should receive her notice. The Workhouse Master's re- port book stated that Nurse Lobley would not take her turn with the nurses in night duty. When the time arrived for her to go on night duty she refused and she was then told that she would be dismissed. She asked to be allowed to go home at once, and permission was granted. She awn packed np her belongings and left the house. The matron had since been in communica- tion with the Northern Nurses Association with the view of obtaining another nurse. The Clerk read a letter h, lad received from Miss Lobley asking for the wages due and also a testimonial. She stated that if they knc w the case thoroughly they would find that night duty was not the only cause for her refusal to do such work. The Clerk stated that he impressed upon her the duty of giving one month's notice the previous Fri- day when the matter came before the House Committee. —Mr. Davies said, as far as his opinion went, Instead of giving her a month's wages she should have given the Guardians a month's wages. A more shabby way of leaving he had not experienced.—Mr. Longton stated that she was not straightforward in her letter when she said there was another grievance and yet did not state that grievance. He moved that the question of a testi- monial be left in the hands of the House Committee.— The motion was carriel—lt was mentioned by the Clerk that the Matron had been in communication with a per- son who had been nurse at Bangor workhouse. Miss Ward would go and see her and if she seemed suitable she would come on duty at once.

===== 0002601_18941103_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
GIFTS.

It was reported that Miss Green, of the Railway Mis- sion, attended the House with a quantity of flowers and distributed them herself. A eift of periodicals was acknowledged from Mr. Iddon, 59, Lovely-lane.

INCREASING THE SALARIES.

The CHAIRMAN (Mr. Houghton) moved—" That in consideration of the near approach of an election of the whole Board under new conditions, the several applica- tions for increase of salaries be referred to the new Board except in the case of the application by Nurse Smale, and that her salary be increased by £2 103. per annum, according to the understanding with her upon her appointment." He said he thought it would be a very wrong thing for that expiring Board U. commit itself by increasing the salaries of officials because they would be permanent charges. The Board would soon be entirely differently constituted, and also in view of the probable reduction in the rateable value of the township he thought the question of salaries would be better dealt with by the new Board. If they lost the townships which had been proposed it would be a very serious thing for the Board, as the burden of expenses would come more on the part of the Union which was left. Ile could hardly forbear from saying that in his opinion their officers belonging to the Union were well paid. There might be a little readjustment necessary but:he considered they were well paid. The ratepayers, he believed, were never in such a had condition to pay the rat9s. Farmers had no sale for their :produce, and what little was being done by manufacturers was in a half-hearted way. It Was the same with commercial reen.—Mr. Hill seconded the resolution.—Mr. Longton moved an amendment that the Board proceed with the consideration of the applica- tions on their men's. He said that was only a respect- able and fair way of dealing with one's servants.—Mr. A. J. Jolley seconded the amendment. No reason, he said, Lad been given him why consideration of the appli- cations should not be given.—Mr. Ratcliffe asked whether they were under an agreement to increase the salaries.—The Chairman said he did not think there was any understanding that the salaries should be increased. —The Clerk said that looking at six or seven advertise- ments for nurses, the lowest salary was £3O a year. That Union paid £3O, but they commenced them at a lower figure, leading them to believe that if they performed their duties satisfactory they would receive an increase of salary. That was so in the case of Nurse Smale. In the other cases the officers expected to get something like their predecessors.—Mr. PILLING referred to the resolution he had given that the salaiv of Mr. Aukland, relieving offieer, be increased from £95 to £lO5. When Mr. Aukland was engaged he was given to understand that the vaccination fees would amount to £5, in addition to his salary of £95 a year as relieving officer. Toe vaccination fees had only amounted to £3. During the time he had been with them he ha l done a great deal of work and had saved the Guardians some- thing like £l5 a year.—Mr. OWEN wished to know whether it was correct that the Guardians had held out sonsething to Mr. Aulcland which he had not received.— The CLERK said Mr. Aukland was given to understand that the vaccination fees would amount to £5 per year, whereas they only amounted to a little over £3. On Mr. Longton's amendment being put to the vote it was carried by 14 voto3 to 9. 'X The applications for increasebf salaries were then dealt with. Mr. PILLING moved his resolution that Mr. Aukland's salary be increase I from £95 to £lO2, in addi- tion to the fees as vaccination officer. Mr. AUKLAND in his apnlication stated that he had effected a saving of £43 12s. 4d. during the past twelve months, in the dis- tribution of relief. He also referred to the fact that the vaccination fees would *come less owing to the Ship Canal people leaving the district.—Mr. HIBBERT seconded Mr. Pilling's resolution.—Mr. LONGTON moved an amend- ment that the salary he fixed at £lOO in ado.ition to the vaccination fees.—Mr. CLARKE seconded.—Mr. W. J. Slia.w and Mr. Gill supported the original motion.—Mr. Longton with the consent of Mr. Clarke withdrew his amendment and Mr. Pilling's motion was agreed to.— Mr. DAVIES moved that the master and matron each receive an increase of £5 in their salaries. making £l2O ler the workhouse master and £6O to the matron. He spoke of the efficient way they discharged their duties, and stated that the increase of salary would only make it the same as their predecessors.—Mr. SHAW seconded. —Mr. K. OWEN moved that the salaries remain as they were.—Mr. HILL seconded.—Mr. GILL stated that on the last occasion when the application was considered he opposed it, and he still thought that tile salaries Mr. and Mrs. Ward received were very good ones. The state of trade was worse now than it was last year and it was hardly the time for raising salaries. The Master had a clerk to assist him at £5O a year, and his predecessor had not, and there were now no schools to look after. When they took into consideration that everything was provided with the exception of clothing, and that there was a salary of £l7O per year between the master and matron he thought they were doing very well and the applica- tion might very well stand over for some other time. The amendment was carried by 12 votes to 10.—Mr. A. J. JOLLEY moved a further resolution that the Matron receive an increase of £5. Mr. RATCLIFFE seconded.— Mr. OWEN moved an amendment that no increase of salary be granted. Mr. LONGTON saconded .—On being pet to the meeting Mr. Jolley's motion was carried by 12 votes to Io.—The Board also increased the salaries of Miss Curry, portess 103., making £3O per year, and Mr. Brobbins, eneine tenter was granted an increase of 21. per week in his wages, making 325. per week. Nurse Smale's salary was increased by £2 103. per year and MeMines' salary as bandmaster was increased from £lO to £l3.

A LETTER FROM A VISITOR.

• Mr. W. Hough, of Knutsford, a Guardian in the Altrincham division, wrote to the Chairman thanking him for the kindness shown to him in allowing him the privilege of inspecting their workhouse, and also allow- ing him to sit in the boardroom whilst transecting their usual business. Thorn was much to admire but there was one thing he did not admire and that was their Boardroom. It appeared to him as a visitor that in the event of a full attendance of members it would not add to their comfort or health, more especially in hot weather. Surely the ratepayers would not begrudge them a comfortable and healthy room in which to transact the huginess of the Union seeing that the Guardians gave their time and service free. He also referred in felicitons: terms to the matron of the house and he was pleased to see the juvenile ap- nearance of their respected clerk (Mr. Sutton) having known him for over a quarter of a century. In con- clusion he stated that he thought a visit of that dis- cription tended to good feeling and assisted each other in their variols duties as Guardians of the poor.

A TIMELY WARNING.

Never trust those who promise too much at a time The patent medicines which pretend to cure "at once," in "a single dose," or in "ten minutes," convince us that they are unnatural in their operation, and that, therefore, either the promise made is a false one, or that the remedy is worse than the disease. It is not "at once " that a man becomes Consumptive ; it is not "at a single meal" that you acquire Indigestion and Dyspepsia ; it is not in " ten minutes " that your Liver becomes sluggish and out of order. No. These com- plaints and others which might be named are gradual in their hold to release you" at once," " in ten minutes or" at a single dose." As the disease, so its cure. Steady and certain is its approach. Steady and certain should be its retreat. It is in this fact that the secret of the wonderful, the unparalleled success of Gwilym Evans Quinine Bitters lies. The more recent the disease the more easily removed. Overwork ought to be overpaid. Nature insists upon payment of her first dues. Ile who comes in a hurry from active work, rapidly swallows his food and hurries out again, is piling up a debt which nature makes him pay in a painful dyspepsic experience. Gwilym Evans's Quinine Bitters will help you to pay, and the sooner you take it the better. Try this .;reat Natural remedy. Try it now. Recovered Patients say it is an Unfailing Remedy. Vernon-st., Southport, was charged with having on the 24th October unlawfully ill-treated a horse by overload- ing the same.—lnspector Luff, R.S.P.C.A., stated that shortly before three o'clock on the date mentioned he was in Sankey-st. when he saw the defendant in charge of a bay gelding drawing a load of ironwork. The horse stopped almost every three yards and the defend- ant was urging it along by the use a whip. He asked the defendant what was the weight of the load to which he replied 21 tons. The horse was breathing very heavily and sweating excessively. He ordered the defendant to procure an adiitional horse, and on the cart and load being weighed, the weight was found to be three tons al cwts.—P.C. Bruen said he was in com- pany with the last witness at the time stated. The horse was scarcely able to move four yards at a time with the load. A fine of ss. and costs was imposed.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

STEALING A MAN'S BOOTS WHILE HE SLEPT. At the Borough Court, on Tuesday morning, Catherine Fallon, Dolman's-lane, Mary Nottingham, Bakehouse- yard, Ellen Price, Bostock-st. and John Brown, Bos- tock-st., were all charged with stealing from the person of Thomas Jones, while in 3, Bakehouse-yard on Mon- day afternoon, a pair of boots value 7s. his property, or receiving the same knowing them to have been stolen. All the prisoners pleaded not guilty.—Mr. Hunt stated that Thomas Jones, the complainant, was a labourer on the Ship Canal, and resided in Barry-street, Latchford. John Bro-.vn, or" Nobby " Brown, works on the canal with Jones. On the day named Jones went to 3, Bake- house-yard, where Nottingham lives and sent for some beer. About five o'clock the complainant !ell asleep and was awakened by somebody trying to force a pair of slippers on his feet. The four plisoners were all stand- ing round but he could not tell which it was who was doing so. His boots were gone, and Brown told him they were pledged and that the pawnticket was in his (Jones') pocket. Jones on looking found that there was a ticket in his pocket.—Thomas Jones, the complainant, stated that at the time these things took place he was under the influence of drink. He corroborated Mr. Hnnt's statement.—An assistant at Morton's pawnshop stated that Fallon had pawned the boots at their shop and said that John Brown had sent her. Price was with her.—D.S. Monaghan stated that at six p.m. on Monday the complainant came to the police station in his stockings and lodged a complaint about the theft. He accordingly went to Bakehouse-yard and brougnt the prisoners to the police court. All the prisoners had had drink purchased with the money got for the boots.—The magistrates inflicted a penalty of 53. or seven days in each case.

MINOR CASES. WARRINGTON BOROUGH POLICE COURT. FRlDAY.—Before Alderman Harrison (chairman), Councillor H. Roberts, Dr. Gornall, and Mr. S. Wright. AN ADSOURNED CASE OF CRUELTY TO CHILDREN.—The adjourned case against John Knight and Katherine Knight was again brought before the magistrates. In- spector McAteer stated that he had visited the defend- ants house and found a great improvement in the condi- tion of the house and the children. Be, therefore,wished to withdraw the case against the defendants. BREACH OF THE FACTORY ACT.—MeSSFS. John Walker and Co., Bewsey-st., were summoned at the instance of the Inspector of Factories with having OD the 21st Sept. employed two women named Mary Jane Hankinson and Margaret Jarvis, without having sent the prescribed notice at the prescribed time. Mr. Percy Davies, solici- tor, appeared on behalf of the defendants. Mr. Rogers, H.M. Inspector of Factories, after .explaining the Acts under which the action was taken, said that on the 21st September, Mr. A. Newlands, factory inspector, visited the defendant's printing works at about 10.5 pm. He found two women who were just ceasing work. They had been at work from eight o'clock the same morning. No notice was sent up until the following day whereas it should have been sent at eight o'clock the same night. As would be seen from the postmark the notice was posted the following day. It was found desirable to insist that notice must be sent the same night before the over- time occurred. The defendants were liable to a penalty of £5 in each ease. Mr. A. Newlands gave corroborative evidence as to visiting the works at the time stated. For the defence Mr. Davies stated that it was a mere techni- cal offence. It had been correctly stated that any person who intended to work persons overtime should send notice of such intention to the Inspector of Factories. On the date named the man whose duty it was to send the notices in, failed to do so at the proper time. The Manager (Mr. Bond) would now see that the notice was sent at the proper time. A fine of ss. and costs in the first case and the costs in the second case was imposed SATURDAY.—Before Mr. J. Burgess (chairman) and Mr. S. Wright. SECOND COURT.

Before Mr. T. H. Pearson (chairman) and Mr. Norman

McCorquodale. A DANGEROUS PRACTICIL—WaIter Carter and John Hazeldine were each charged with unlawfully and feloni- ously placing stones in some railway points at Old Fold Colliery, belonging to Messrs. Richard Evans and Co., on Oct 15.—Mr. Stuart who prosecuted stated that a platelayer in the employ of the Company, named John Stockport had cleaned out the points on the morning of the above date, and a little afterwards on going to the same place, he saw the points" half and half " with pieces of coal and stones laid between them. The two defendants who were near by, ran away when they saw Stockport. P.C. Addison was informed, and the de- fendants were arrested. When charged, Carter said that he held the lever while Hazeldine put the pieces of coal between, and Hazeldine also made the same statement. The danger of the defendants' action could be shown by the fact that a quarter of an hour after the stones were discovered, a traira with 42 waggons went over the spot, and had not the stones been taken out, they would all have been thrown off the line, and p,,,rhaOs loss of life wonld have ensued.—Corroborative evidence was given by Stockport, P.C. Addison, and Mr. Latham, manager oh the colliery, the latter of whom said that the damage which would have occurred had the waggons gone off the line, would have been £l5O, besides danger and perhaps loss of life.—ln reply to the manistrates, it was stated that Carter was 14 and Hazeldine 15 years of age, and as they were too old to be birched, they were sent to goal for 14 days' hard labour.

RUGBY.

WARRINGTON v. SWINTON. •

The first meeting of these two teams this season took place at Wilderspool on Saturday. It will be remem- bered that Warrington were the only team in the Cup Competition of last year to account for the Swintonians, for they beat them at Wilderspool by a goal and a try to nil, and at Swinton each team scored a try. This season, however, the "Lions" are doing very badly,and it was, therefore, generally anticipated that they would succumb to Warrington, who had done so well at Wigan the previous Saturday, whereas Swinton on the same ground a few weeks previously had to submit to a crushing defeat. About 6,000 spectators • lined the ground on Saturday to witness the encounter, and Mr. Slavin, of Wigan, held the position of master o cere- monies. Each side had a representative team, Hampson for the first tune donning the Warrington jersey. The following was the composition of the teams :—Warring- ton: Boscow, full back; Cunningham, Potts, Hampson, and Burton, three-quarters ; J. 'l'. Bate and Clark, half ! hacks; Turner, Jolley, Nevins, Thornilev, Dakin, Berry, Taylor and Buckley, forwards. Swinton: Winter. bottom, full back; Marsh, Valentine, Crews, and Lewis, threequarters ; Pearson, and Harris, half- backs: Murray, Knowles, Sharpies, Evans, Harrington, Cleminson, Harris, and Tickley, forwards. Valentine won the tos., and set Warrington to defend the pavilion goal. Nevins kicked off, and Marsh's exchange was capitally returned to the quarter flag by Boscow. The home forwards screwed the subsequent scrimmage in fine style, and were well on for the Swinton line, when Pearson was fortunate in kicking in touch. Not to be denied Warrington continued to press, but ever eager- ness spoiled their efforts, and only a minor resulted. Murray's kick out was followed by Marsh who securing raced away to be pulled up by Boscow. Hampson now had a sprint,and his effort was supplemented by Burton, but Pearson breaking away, made a grand dribble-almost three parts the field, where he was obstructed by Hampson. Valentine took the penalty kick which was given, and kicked wide, only a minor resulting. This invasion of the home territory did not last long, and Nevins gained a great amount of ground by wriggling through his opponents. Burton gained further ground, and then Dakin securing got over near the posts, but was called back on the ground that he was offside. A penalty kick was allowed Warringtan immediately after- wards in front of the goal posts, but it was not taken advantage of, and a long spell of give and take play followed. Boscow was loudly cheered for repelling an onslaught and getting his kick under very great diffi- culties. Following the kick, Warrington raced away and a fins bout of passing ensued between Bate, HemPsee and Burton ; the latter being pushed into touch near the scoring point. Two minors tell in quick succession to Warrington, and Swinton were penned in, when suddenly Pearson got away and dribbled down the field. When that player reached Boscow tie passed to Valentine, and that player in turn to Lewis who got over against the right goal post. A confident appeal was made on the ground that the ball had been knocked forward, but the point was allowed. Valen- tine's kick at goal was a miserable failure. Warrington had hard lines in not getting over after this reverse, but only gained a barren minor, and half time soon after- wards arrived with Swinton leading by a try. At the commencement of the second half, Swinton had slightly the best of matters, for Valentine's kick off was poorly returned, and Boscow stopped Pearson's progress just in time. Cunningham soon after almost got over at the opposite end, and Winterbottom almost let Clark in by mulling the ball. The attack was brought to an end by another minor—the fifth—being conceded to Warrington. These repeated disappointments seemed to damp the ardour of the Warrington men for a time, and Swinton had a look in, but found the defence ton good. A mark to Burton was the means of the sixth minor being conceded although it was a near thing for a drop goal. At length an irresistible rush was made by the Warrington forwards and ended in Jolley equalising near the corner, amid deafening cheers. Burton almost increased the try to a goal, Each side now tried to get ahead, and play was exciting in the extreme, and each side was visited in turn. Pearson for Swinton, and Cunningham and Burton for Warrington had extremely hard lines. Warrington were now deprived of Clarke's services for a time owing to an injury, and they prac- tically played with 14 men to the cl.se of the game. The last five minutes found Warrington constantly on the attack. Time after time they appeared to be on the point of scoring, but could not. The last minute now arrived and Swinton appeared to be gaining ground, and everybody had concluded that the game would end in a draw, when the whole Warrington team made a last desperate rush, and Buckley successfully grounded the ball near the same place that the last try was gained. The scene which followed was one of the most remark- able ever witnessed on the ground for hats waved and the applause was tremendous. They were again raised when Burton kicked a goal. The restart had scarcely been made when the final arrived leaving Warrington victors of an remarkable game by a goal and a try to a try. WARRINGTON A v. SWINTON A.

RUNCORN PETTY SESSIONS.

MONDAY.—Before Messrs. James Handley and S. Taylor. RUNCORN FISHMONGERS AT VARIANCE.—George Duckett, fishmonger, of Ckurch-street, was summoned in two instances for committing assaults upon his son, George Cornelius Duckett, and Margaret Glynn.—Mr. Allen appeared for the defendant.—A. number of wit- nesses were called, and from the evidence they gave it would appear that defendant's son, who is 23 years of age, left his father some eighteen months ago and is now married to a sister of the female complainant. Both complainan are also mongers, and a great deal of unp:easantness has existed between them since the son left defendant and was married. The strained relationship was brought to a head on the morning of the 27th inst., when Margaret Glynn and the defendant arrived at Runcorn station le7 the 8.40 a.m, train from Liverpool. A quantity of fish was in the train for both these persons, and George Cornelius Duckett met Mar- garet Glynn on the station to assist her to unload it. During the progress of unloading, the defendant and his son began to quarrel and the defendant, it was alleged, ran at his son and struck him several times. Margaret Glynn held the defendant's right arm and thus enabled the son to get out of his father's grasp. Defendant then turned upon the female complainant and struck her twice.—Mt. Allen, for the defence, said that the son began to jeer at his father when on the station and pre- vented him from getting his fish away at the proper moment. His client was not guilty of either charge. ie attempted to strike his son but could not.—Defendant was fined 53, and 233. 6d. costs for the assault upon his son, and ss, and us. 61 costs for the assault upon Mrs. Glynn. UNMUZZLED DOGS.—George Shone, Alfred Stanway, Thomas Parry, and William Sutton were each ordered to pay 4s. 61. .eests for allowing their dogs to stray abroad without muzzles in contravention of the Rabiea (Muzzling of Dogs) Or.ler. SQUABBLING RELATIONS.—David Newport, of Massey's- court, was summoned by his sister-in-law, Mary Wool- gar, 24, Wivern-place,for assaulting her on the 27th Oct. —The complainant said that at about ten o'clock on the night of the day named she went to defendant' s house to speak to her sister, who was his wife. She opened the door, and as her sister was not to be seen in the room she asked defendant, who was sitting in a chair with his back towards her, where she was. He immedi• ately got up,obtained some water,and threw it over tier. At that moment her sister came in and giving her a dish of water, told her to throw it over him, her hus- band. This she would not do, however. The defend- ant then ran at her and abused her.—Evidence was given to the effect that the complainant was found in a bleeding condition with marks upon her throat and fore- head.—Defendant denied that he had struck the com- plainant and alleged that she had come to the house with two other women for the purpose of "having a row." He admitted throwing some water over them to drive them away. The complainant threw a dish at him, and would have thrown a lamp also, hut the door was shut against her just in tune.—Fined 10s. including costa.

Re examined It the prwious day and it was then suffering no pain whatever, neither was the foot hot. The only thing which made the mare appear lame was that it had a peculiar gait. WARRINGTON L. AND N.W. ALBION'S V. PARR VICTORIA. —On Saturday, at the Arpley Meadows. the London and North-Western Albions had for their opponents Parr Victoria. The Parr full backs could not be compared with Griffiths and Jones of the Albions, the former being noticeable for the effective way he dealt with the rustles of the visitors, and the latter for his clean kicking. The Albions as yet hold an unbeaten certificate.

THE DEPARTURE OF THE REV. J. W. VARLEY.

In the Baptist schoolroom, Latchford, on Thurs- day last, a tea and meeting were held in order to bid farewell to the pastor. About 100:persons sat down to tea, the tables being presided over by the following ladies :—Mrs. Davids in, Mrs. Julian, Mrs. Hunt, Mrs. Fairhurst,, Mrs. P. Wilkinson Mrs. Varley, Mrs. Anams,Mrs Cliffe, Mrs. W. T. Wilkinson, Miss Wells, and Miss Eyes. •

After the lifting of the cloth a meeting was held, pre- sided over by Mr. George Wells (senior deacon of the church), and there were also on the platform :—The Revs. J. W. Varley, J. S. Hughes, E. T. Reed, J. War- nock, M.A., 8.D., J. Fowler (Manchester), C®uncillor H. Roberts, Messrs. Joseph Alhinson, sen., W. Davidson, and W. Adams. Mr. Wilkinson (secretary) read letters of apolegy or non-attendance from the Revs. J. Yonge, E. T. Moore, D. W. Roberts, J. Kirtlan, G. D. Mason, W. J. Kirkland, and C. Clay (Lymm). The CHAIRMAN said they were met there that night to bid farewell to Mr. Varley. He had worked with him there for over eight years,and during that time they had worked with a spirit of co-operation. For the first time in the history of that church they were holding a fare- well meeting to their pastor. That church had been in existence for over 42 years,and during the whole of that time it had only had two pastors. (Applause.) There was only one chureh in the district besides that which was able to say it. During the nine years that Mr. Varley had been their pastor he had never swerved from preaching the gospel of Jesus Christ. He was of the opinion that they would have greater difficulty to fill up the vacancy than they had ten years ago. (Applause.) Rev.J. FOWLER (Manchester) delivered a short address, and said he was very glad for St. Anne's that they were going to have Mr. Varley. Rev. J. S. HUGHES expressed his regret that Mr. Varley, who was his nearest neighbour in that district, was going away so soon after his (Mr. Hughes) commence- ment of his pastorate at Hill Cliffe. He knew Mr. Yarley when he first entered college as a student, then an intelligent, round-faced youth. (Laughter.) The Bishep of Chester had recently stated that Baptist minis- ters were exotics. He daresay they were of that kind but in the case of Mr. Varley he seemed to have taken well to the soil and thrived well amongst them at Latch- ford. He had great admiration for the work he had done. Someone had said that every minister made a fool of himself in his first pastorate. He did not think that after the nine years service of Mr. Varley there. that that was the case with him. He had discharged faithfully, loyally and intelligently the duties of minister of that church. As representing the Baptist denomina- tion of the district, he could testify to the work he had done for their denominition, work which did not appear on the surface. He had done work for their association and it was also to him that they owed the labours and arrangements of their missionary services year after year in that district. He (Mr. Hughes) could enter somewhat into the feelings of Mr. Varley. Latch- ford was his first love, and there he had had his first experiences. He wished him every blegsing and success in the' work to which he was going. (Applause.) Rev. E. T. REED stated that he had come there at some personal inconvenience in order to show his sympathy with Mr. Varies', who had associated himself in various public works in connection with the social, moral and spiritual life of the community. He (Mr. Reed) always received at his hands kindly Christian courtesy and he thought he might say brotherly love. They had been associated together in that grand movement, which was doing so much for the young people of the church, the Young People's Society of Christian Endeavour, and the other day he had the privilege of listening to' one of the best papers he hal heard, dealing with a branch of that delivered by Mr. Vasley. hUe believed it would greatly encourage Mr. Varley to see that during the months they might be without a settled pastor the work there still went forward. He had much joy in wishing him great success in winning souls. (Applause.)

• Councillor H. IlonEnTs said they all felt that they were losing a personal friend, a good preacher, and a sound man in Mr. Varley. A good deal had been said from a clerical point of view that night, and they saw that there was a sort of trades unionism amongst parsons. (Laughter) It was quite customary when birds of a feather got together to say the same thiag of each other. But he was bound to say that what had been said by the gentlemen of the black cloth had been square and was true for they knew it themselves. Well he was sorry that they were losing their pastor,because since he came to Latchford that little chapel had been his house of refuge several times,and it had always been a great pleasure to listen to Mr. Varley's sermons. He had never on a single occasion regretted the time spent in their chapel. He liked Mr. VA Hey for one thing and that was his intense manliness. There was no mistaking what he meant. He knew what he meant and said what he meant, and they might take it that Mr. Varley's convictions would not be concealed. It did not en down with everybody but in these days and in that locality, it was very neces- sary to say it. There would be rejoicing in some quarters in Warrington for ths departure ef Mr. Varley, but he hoped that rejoicing would be very short. He wanted them to feel the responsibility that rested with them in finding a man who ceuld net only take Mr. Varley's place in the pulpit, but a man who could take his place out- side the pulpit, which was more important. Every minister who was worth his salt had a much larger sphere than the four wane of his church. (Hear, hear.) He did not expect them all to agree with him in that,but in these days if a minister of the esosnel ceased in any sense to be a citizen, in his opinion he was not acting up to the teaching of the Bible and the magnificent ex- ample set by his Master. For the young people there he could enter into their feelings because /se remembered many rears wee losing a pastor ht Wycliffe Church,—a man he thought he very nearly adored—the Rev. G. S. Reaney. a man who had the tact of eetting hold of the young people of the church. He honed they would have some comfort in the fact that Mr. Varies' was not going to a smaller, but a larger place, that his opportunities for usefulness and far the public weal generally would he enlarged and intensified. Something had been said abet Mr. Varley's wife. He did net know what the gentlemen on the platform thought, but he was of the opinion that it was far more difficult to select a suitable pastor's wife than for the church to select a pastor. There were few pastors' wives who fulfilled all that they were expected to fulfil in the church. in Mrs. Varley's case he knew she had been a model minister's wife. They were lesSeea great deal in Mrs. Varley. He wished them r.v.•-c.• success and prosperity. (Applause.) r. LIIINSON (treasurer of the church) then handed to Mr. Varley on behalf of the congregation a beautiful silver tea and coffee service,which it may be stated was supplied by Messrs. E. and A. Eustance,ef Sankey-street. Mr. Alhinson referred to the gssod work done by Mr. and Mrs. Varley, stating that the former had served his apprenticeship with them. Rey. J. W. VARLEY in replying said there were some things that were better felt than said, and he thought they would understand that when he came tolook upon the token of their kindness and love, that it was difficult on his part after so many years of service to adequately express his feelings. He wanted te thank them very heartily and very lovingly for what they had given and for the very kind expressions that they had uttered to- wards himself and Mrs. Varley. One recognised that in the work of the ministry it was not only the pastor but the pastor's wife that had to do with the success of the church's work. An uncomfortable home very often meant a bad time in the pulpit. If the home life was not what it should be the minister's work could not be done as it should he done. He would not like that op- portunity to pass without, saving how much of his happiness and of his usefulness had come from the love and sympathy and general helpfulness of his wife. (Applause.) After nine years of service there one could not possibly separate without feelines of sadness. It was like tearing up the roots. There had been a diffi. culty on his part to make no his mind. Is had bee Simple- because he felt that it was Christ's will that he should leave that place and co to St. Anne's that he was enine. He did not know if he was revealing any con- fidences when he said that it was not the flit opner- tunity that he had had of moving from Lateliford although they had never known. It was the third time that he had been asked to leave that pastorate, but in the other cases in no sense did the call seem to be a divine call. He had no hesitation in saving that he believed the present call, God's call to a lareser sphere and to meet difficulties he had never met. in that place. At St. Anne's there had been six years of unpleasantness between Christian people, of secessions, and difficulties which ha' i arisen owing to this slcession. He had been asked to face them and try to c:mouer them because in the opinien of those capable of judging they believed it was possihle for him to bring about that harmonious sympathy, Irma and service which ware inseparable from the work of a useful church. There might have been times during his stay there when it was necessary for him to sneak straight and take a stand on behalf of the whole of the members of that church, hut he had that feeling that he was net leaving them because they wanted him to leave. It is a new experience for him and for them, and he trusted that they would find that the step he was taking was for the benefit of that church, and for the benefit of the church at St. Anne's, He shoull like to thank all the workers who had been so faithful and had co-operated with him in so many kinds of Christian service His pastorate at Latchford would be remembered with gratefulness as long as he lived. ( Annie use.) Mr. JEPSON afterwards spoke. The Rev. J. WARNOCK said he felt it a pririlege to be there and witness the beautiful spirit which existed between pastor and people. He thought it spoke welt • for the heartiness of the congregation when so many were present on an evening like that. He had known Mr. Varley ever since he came to Latchford and had always found him to be one of the most honourable and meet brotherly of men. He was sorry he was leaving the neighbourhood and hoped they would see his faoP from time to time. He wished both Mr. and Mrs. Varley much success in their new sphere, Mr. DAVIDSON and Mr. ADAMS, deacons, also spoke. It may be stated that the Rev. Walter Ssely,"ll' Littlehorouela, near Nuneaton, Warwickshire, will tem- porarily carry on the pa:storel duties at Letchfcrd, until a successor to Mr. Varley is appointed.

Tanner, Messrs. Joseph Knowles (vice-chairman). C. T. Whitley, E. Aaron, S. Woodward, J. Brandreth, T. Lewis, E. Bratherton, T. Baxter, C' .C. J. M. Williams, H. E. Heywood, J. Whitlow, J. Parr, C.
