THE SMALL-PDX HOSPITAL AT MOORE.

The Clerk produced a letter hem Mr. . Dunlop, of the Land Office of the Manchester Ship Canal Company, enquiring how much longer the Authority were likely to require the temporary small-pox hospital, and whether they claimed any portion of the building. He was not in a position to offer a site for a permanent hospital.— Mr. Williams said that he had heard that Mr. Evans, the agent of Sir Richard Brooke, had recently called at the hospital and inspected it. From a statement that had been made to him (Mr. Williams) he understood that on that occasion Mr. Evans had said that he intended taking the hospital before Christmas. Under such cir- cumstances, although Mr. Williams would not advise the Authority to place much credence upon the rumour that Mr. Evans had secured the hospital, he thought it would be well if he were instructed to call upon Mr. Dunlop at his office in Manchester, for the purpose of talking the matter ,over.—Mr. Baxter remarked that at the last meeting of the Public Health Committee of the Cheshire County Council the subj,ct of providing isolation hos- pitals was discussed, and there being a feeling that hospitals were undesirable in little districts the county medical officer was instructed to re,porb as to the neces- sity for them in many parts of Cheshire, and how many. The idea of making groups ot Unions in order to provide proper hospitals met with some favour, but, of course, he did not know what the action of the Council would be. There was no doubt, however, that the Medical Officer would very shortly make his report and it would then be decided if hospitals should be erected.—After some discussion it was resolved that a reply be sent to Mr. Dunlop that the hospital would be retaitval for twelve months, and that Mr. Williams be empowered to make such arrangements as were necessary with Mr. Dunlop.

THE SEWERING OF HELSEY,

The mirmtea of the meeting of the committee, appointed to consider the letter of the Local Government Board with reference to the sewerage of Helshy, held on October 20th, showed that the Clerk read the com- munication in question, produced the plans of the sewers mentioned therein, after which the committee, the Medical Officer of Ffealth,and the Inspector of N‘iisances made a careful exmelnation of the sewers and sewer- outfall. It was subsequently resolved that that part of the sewers from Ebenezer Terrace to the rear of Rake House, which was an open ditch, be piped with inch glazed socket pipes from Mrs. Pritchard's property to the sewer in Rake-lane, and that the sewerage flom that property be diverted to the newly constructed sewer. That a settling tank on the most approved plan for purifyinc.r the sewage be constructed, and that the effluent therefrom be conveyed along the sewer on the north-westerly . side of the railway to the "Flashinv,s," hich communicate with the town. That Mr. Thornley be requested to acquiese in the construction of the shove works, and to construct a sewer from his own premises so as to communicate with one in Rake-lane. That the Inspector of Nuisances obtain estimates for the works proposed, and that the Clerk inform the Local Govern- ment Board what was intended to be done, and also point nut that the annual reports of the Medical Officer show that Helsbv had been an exceptionally healthy district, during the past year, and comparatively free from those diseases which result from insanitary defects. —The minutes were approved.

INCREASE OF SCARLET FEVER.

Dr. Adams, the medical officer of health, reported that three cases of scarlet fever had broken out at Weston Point, three at Norley, fonr at Kingsley, two at Hatch- mere in the township of Kingswood, eight at Weston, and three at Rock Savage, Clifton. In a great many of these cases the children had attended school. The medical officer had visited them and had issued the usual preventive orders.

CAT PROVERBS.

A Cheshire cat. He grins like a Cheshire cat. Cheese was formerly sold in Cheshire moulded like a cat. The allusion is to the grinning cheese-cat, but is applied to persons who show their teeth and gums when they laugh. A Kilkenny cat. The story is that, during the rebellion of Ireland, Kilkenny was garrisoned by a troop of Hessian soldiers, who amused themselves in barracks by tying two cats together by their tails and throwing them across a clothes-line to fight. The officers, hearing of this, resolved to put a stop to the practice. The look- out man, enjoying the sport, did not observe the officer on duty approaching the barracks; but one of the troopers, more quick sighted, seizing a sword, cut the two tails, and the cats made their escape. When the officer inquired the meaning of the two bleeding tails,he was coolly told that two cats had been fighting and had devoured each other all but the tails.—" The Dictionary of Phrase and Fable."

Manufactured only at 78, NEW OXFORD STREET, LONDON.

THE GOVERNMENT AND THE AMNESTY OF IRISH PRISIONERS.

1 deputation from the Corporation of Dublin waited on Monday upon the Cilia Secretary to plead for amnesty for the political prisioners. The deputation based their appeal solely on the ground of mercy. Mr. Morley, in reply, said that the decision with regard to these prisioners had been come by the whole Cabinet, and he ad no desire to shirk his personal responsibility in the matter. He recognised no claim of these men for special or extraordinary clemency. He did not like to use the word" never "—a bad word in state- manship. The time might come when, in compliance with the universal desire of the Irish people, their cases might again be considered, and he for his part would be ready to reconsider the question with every desire to make concessions to a current of popular sentiments in Ireland which, he had to admit, was not without sorre justification on the past history of the country.

===== 0002601_18941103_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. BRUNNER AND THE HOUSE OF

LORDS.

A largely attended public meeting was held at Northwich on Monday evening, under the auspices of the Northwich Division Liberal Association. Mr. Henry Bratt, J.P., presided, and was supported by Mr. J. T. Brunner, M.P., and Mr. J. liowlands, M.P. for East Finsbury.—Dr. Hewitt, J.P., proposed a resolution to the effect that the meeting learned with satisfaction the definite position taken up by the Prime Minister at Bradford, and heartily assured him of its earnest support in the coming struggle to abolish the veto of the House of Lords. The Government was also thanked for the democratic legislation it had already passed.—Mr. Edward Milner, C.C., seconded. Mr. Brunner, in supporting the resolution, said he joined sincerely in the desire that the veto of the House of Lords should be abolished. There was not very much that was left of it, which followed from the fact that by resolution of the House of Commons, thrice repeated, the Lords were unable to make anr charge upon the people. The Lords could not touch their taxa- tion nor their local rating. In no way whatever could they interfere with the will and the decision of the House of Commons in any question relating to a charge upon the people. On a question of peace and war the representatives of the people were supreme. On the whole question of the government of the nation as far as money was concerned the House of Commons was supreme. The House of Lords could not appoint an official, except an unpaid one, without the leave of the people's representatives. They could not add a gun or a man to her Majesty's forces. On all these questions— and they were of the last importance—on all these ques- tions of foreign politics, of war, of payment of taxation, whether imperial or local, the representatives of the people were supreme. (Cheers.) They were now asked to declare that not only on matters of money, but on all matters that concerned their lives, their happiness, and their good government, their representatives should be supreme. (Cheers). What a condition of affairs it was that 560 men in the House of Lords, the origin of whose appearance there was in some cases to be acknowledged with shame, should be equal to the rest of her Majesty's subjects. There were in the United Kingdom, accord- ing to last year's register, 8,229,000 electors, and if they divided that number by 560 they would find that each peer was equal to 11,143 of his fellow men. (Laughter.) Was there a man amongst them so unmanly, so craven, as to allow that any man, no matter what his origin or rank might be, was equal to 11,000 men, that he had 11,000 times as much care for his neighbours, as much intelligence, or as much patriotism ? (Cries of " He agreed with Lord Rosebery, who said on Saturday that he could cunt upon the house of Commons and upon the country,and he was ready to send to the Prime Minister from that meeting a message that he might count upon them as a part of the country. (Cheers.) He prophesied that when the opinion of the country upon that great question was obtained there would be no fifth act ot the drama needed, but that it could finish with a very triumphant second act. (Loud cheers.) Mr. Rowlands, M.P., and others spoke, and the reso- lution was carried, as was also one expressing confidence in Mr. Brunner.

THE HOUSE OF LORDS.

Sir,—The abolition of the House of Lords being one of the leading questions of the day,will you kindly allow me to make a few remarks on the subject. I hold Mr. Cha- mberlain mainly responsible for the Lords' rejection of the Horne Rule Bill, &c., because he is the aider and abettor of Salisbury in his policy. I think it is about time we had done with Lord Rule in England, for their rule means the benefiting of their own order. Then, again, the Lords talk a gr .1 deal about the separation of Ire- land from England if we grant Home Rule, but I believe the separation they most dread is the separation of the rack-rents of the tenants of Ireland from their pockets.— Yours truly, It. B.

STOCKTON HEATH.

THE SHIVERING OF CHESTER ROAD.—On Tuesday afternoon at the fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority, Mr. J. Mclntyre presiding, the minutes of the last meeting of the Appleton Parochial Committee, held on the 26th October in the Bridge Inn, ,Appleton, were submitted for confirmation. They showed that the Clerk had read a letter from the Local Government Board sanctioning the further loan of £215 for works of sewering in Chester-road, such sum to be borrowed on the credit of the rates and to be repaid with interest within a period of 30 years from the date of the borrowing. The Clerk reported that he had pre- pared and forwarded the draft of the contract for 'executing the work to Messrs. Sayce and Randle for their approval, and had applied to the Public Works Loan Commissioners for the loan.—The minutes were con- firmed on the proposition of Mr. Joseph Knowles.

BurrEnsimusc CLASSES.—Under the auspices of the Cheshire County Council, classes in butter-making were held in the well-appointed dairy of Mr. Shakeshaft, Crouchley Farm, conducted by Miss Bowda,ge, of the Dairy Institute, Worleston, on Tuesday,Wednesday,and Thursday, last week. The attendance on the first day was 32, on the second, 31, and on the last day over 60. The cl.sses were very successful, and if the weather had not been so stormy it is very probable that there would have been arnuch larger gathering. FRODSHAM.

Tax FRODSHAII WATERWORICS.—At the fortnightlY meeting of the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority held on Tuesday afternoon at Dutton Workhouse,the minutes of the previous meeting of the Frodsham Parochial Corn- tnittee. which took place op the 27th inst., were sub- mitted for confirmation. From the minutes it appeared that a letter had been read from the Local Gov ‘rnment Board with the scale of charges for the consumption of water from the Sanitary Authority's mains, and it was arrane'ed that the same be printed and delivered to con- sumers and others at a charge of threepence per copy. The Clerk reported that he had visited London with regard to the additional loan required for the compla,. tion of the water scheme, and had had an interview with the representatives of the Local Government Board. He produced and read the formal sanction of that body to a further expenditure and loan of £7OO, which was considered satisfactory. It was understood that certain local builders would have the option of tendering for the works at the pumping st?tion.—lt was also arranged that the duties of the superintendent engineer recommence from that date until the works were complete. It was pointed out that the superinten- -ding engineer had ceased to act on the 30th September. A communication was read from the Fr.: isliam Gas and Water Company with reference to the use of incandes- cent burners in the street lamps, and as it was found that it would entail an additional sum of £lO 10s., the committee anowed the matter to drop.—A discussion afterwards ensued on the new urban bye laws, which were approved by the committee. The Authority adopted the minutes. ALLEGED BIGAMY.—At Bolton, last week, John Henry Carter, late of Rochdale. was committed to the Assiz for bigamously marrying Fanny Rachel Blackburn in July, 1E92, his wife, to whom he was married in 1839, being alive. The prisoner left his wife at Frodshain, went to Horwich, where he lodged with Blackburn, and represented himself as a widower, sayirag that his wife •was buried at Salford Cemetery. THEFT OF CARRoTs.—John Darlington, 13 Years of age, was charged with stealing a quantity of carrots, valued is. which were (Towing in enclosed land at Fred- sham Lordship on the 21th September.—James Andrews the owner of the stolen vegetables and who resides at Mickle Dale, said that for some time his carrots have been stolen and he was now compelled to take these pro- ceedings in order to stop such petty larcenies. He de- posed to catching the accused with carrots in his pos- session. The defendant was with another boy,and they were both on his (prosecutor's) land.—Owing to Mr. Andrews stating that he did not wish to press the case, the defendant was discharged upon payment of costs (4s. 6c1.) and warned as to his future conduct. ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

The traditions which hover round the meetings of the Swinton and Warrington clubs were kept up on Satur- day, and again the home team were successful. It is a very singular circuri -lance that in the ten previous matches to Saturdays,each team had won four,lost four, and drawn two. The wins had been on the home ground on every occasion, and there had been also one draw on each ground. Swinton had however a slight advantage in the fact that they had scored six goals, eight tries, whereas, Warrington had only scored five goals, six tries.

Warrington is truly a thorn in the side of Swinton, for last year, Swinton wont through the whole of the- competition up to the last match but one,withont defeat when they had to encounter Warrington, and had to retire dvfeated by a goal and a try to nil, thus robbing them of their championship to which they would have been entiticl otherwise, by a superior average of points. Warrington actually claimed three out of four possible points from Swintoa last year.

The Swinton " Lions " have fallen upon evil times this year. owing to various changes in the personnel of their team. For instance, the international, F. P. Jones, has returned to his first love, New Brighton, and Valentine, although still a brilliant player, is not as good as he once was. The result has becs that this far they have not won a single match in the competition, and have only drawn two. In fact they now hold the lowest place in the competition. This is a downfall indeed, and one which all good sportsmen would wish to see quickly altered. It would be a pity to see this once mighty team fighting for its place in the Le .gue at the end of the season.

The match on Saturday wra of a much more exciting description than the last one seen on the ground, and notwithstanding the low position Swinton occupy on the league table, they drew a larger " ,rate " than usual, there being about 5,000 present. The Warrington team completely overplayed their opponents at the commence- ment of the game, but as they did not put the requisite finishing touches on, they made no score, and there was great consternation when Lewis got over near the goal posts, for Swinton. There was a suspicion of a knock forward before Lewis got the ball, but the try was allowed nevertheless. Valentine would not do amiss if he would practise a little Rugby goal kicking. for he evidently thought that he was playing aissocietion, and his kick sect the ball below instead of above the bar.

The Swintonians having obtainel the lead of a try kept this until the middle of the second half, and there was an ominous silence on the field ,but this was broken, when Jolley was able to equalise. This was however nGthing compared to the shQut which ensued when in the last minute of the match Buckley scored the winning point, and Burton placed the result beyond all doubt by kicking a goal. This ending was totally unlonked for, as play appeared to have settlei down in one place. It was in fact a last desperate charge upon the enemy's fortress which carried the day. • Such an ending will long be remembered by those who witnessed it, and Buckley and Burton are to be congratulated upon it.

The result fairly represented the difference of the two teams, for Warrington were superior in almost every department, the half-back division being the most equally matched. Bocow played a grand game, and but for his clevernees, Swinton might have gained more than one try, Winterbottom at the other end alEo played a good game. The Warrington threequarter line was stronger than usual, for Hampson made a most promising debut in first class football. Burton and he worked well together as they know each other's play. They were however too much neglected. They had not a fair chance of showing what they could do, Nr almost all the play was at the other end. Potts was too reluc- tant in his passing, and thus lost many chances of putting his side on the aggressive, which he might have done by a timely pass. Cunningham had a tremendous amount of work, and he got through it very creditably. The half-backs as I before mentioned were very evenly matched, but Pearson was probably the best.

The Warrington forwards to a man worked bard, and it was entirely through their exertions that each try was obtained. They appeared to overthrow all opposition on each occasion when they got over, and it will be noticed that it wr • a forward on each occasion that scored, viz., Jolley and Buckley. The Warrington team have secured a reli-ble goalzkicker at length in the per- son of Burton, who when playing for St. Marv' s was a very sure kick. He was able, on Saturday, notwith- standing the darkness to kick a goal from a difficult angle, and this was the first Warrington have gained in any of the League fixtures.

The League is becominc, very interesting, for the hitherto invincible Oldham team, who a fortnight ago beat a team by two goals, thirteen tries to nil, were beaten on Saturday by the bottom club of the League— Rochdale Hornets. Wigan also met with defeat, and as a consequence Warrington are level with Wigan in points. and only one point behind Oldham and Tyldes- lev. Our local team are therefore not ewt of the running yet by any means. The following is tho posi- tion of the League table up to date:—

SWIMMING.

A more successful gala than the one held at the Cnr. poration Baths, Legh-st., could hardly have wished for The building wag completely crowded, the handicaps were well patronized, and the other exhibitions were excellent. There were 60 entries for one handicap and 32 for the other. E. A. Jones for Warrington swam exceptionally well, winning in his heat and coming in 4th in the 100 yards final. The most exciting race of the evening was of course the one in which J. H. Tyeis and R. Sbarrack took part. For the first three lengths of this heat Sharrack with 3 secs. start seemed to be having it all his own way until the 4th and last length when Tyers amid terrific applause drew ahead and came in a good first. A disappointment was in store however for later on it was announced that Tyers could not swim in the final as he had to leave for an engagement at the Manchester Swan. Throughout the heats both St. Helens and Warrington men made a good show. Master W. H. Curwen astonished the spectators with his clever fancy swimming performance which would have put to shame many an older swim- mer. He is well built for his 11 Years and shows both confidence and skill in his exhibition. Nuttall. though unsuccessful in his attempt to beat Tyers' 250'vds. record swam very strongly and received a deserved ovation of applause. In his long plunge or port,. from the diving- boari J. McHugh gave strong evidence of his power as a distance diver. The event of the evening was, of course, the final league contest between St. Helens and Warring- ton. On entering the bath it was noticed that in point of weight the two teams were about equal and for the first ten minutes We .rington, in the shallow end,pushed their opponents hard, causing some consternation among the visitors by scoring twice without any return. Then St. Helens got one in and on the change of sides the St. Helens men showed their superiority and ran out victors by three goals to tw.. Though beaten the Warrington men could hardly have played bett .r. The man they had to fear most was Sharrnck, who, being a county man, was in advance of the local team by his q nick and long shooting. Without him it is very doubtful if the "one man team," as it is called, would have returned home the possessors of the cup. Scotson, Locker and Turner played with a eir accustomed dash bat were too well watched to do much in the scoring line, though Locker shot both the Warrington goals. The combina- tion in both teams was excellent, each man being "marked " throughout the match by his sipponent.

DEATH OF THE CZAR.

The Emperor of Russia died at Yalta on Thursday afternoon. Intelligence of the sad event was transmitted to all the capitals of Europe, and was received every- where with feelings of regret. The late Czar was born at St. Petersburg in 1845,and succeeded his father when that Monarch was assassinated by Nihilists in 1881. In 1866 he married the Princess Dagmar of Denmark, sister of the Princess of Wales. He is succeeded by his eldest son, the Grand Duke Nicholas, who was born at St. Petersburg in 1868, and is betrothed to Princess Mix of Hesse, a granddaughter of Queen Victoria.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 3,

SUDDEN DEATH.

On Monday Morning, Mr. Owen, sen., of Bank-st. died very suddenly. He had been declining in health from some time past, having reached an advanced age, but he was not confined to his bed. Immediately he came down stairs on Monday morn- ing he fell to the ground,and on the members of the household going to him they found that life was extinct.

About seven o'clock on Monday night an unusual scene was witnessed in Church-st. It appears that a man carrying a gun was going along Church-st. land when opposite Menrs. Rylands Bros'. works from some unexplained cause the charge exploded, and a bullet grazed the cheek of Mrs. Thornley,of, Greenall-st. After full explanations hnd been Oven they were both allowed to go home. Beyond the temporary fright and shock the woman was uninjured. ALL HOUSFHOLD LINEN', hemmed and marked, free of charge. —.T. and W. DurroN. Sankev-st., Warrington.—Advt. LECTURE AT BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHAPEL._

On Monday evening, a lecture was given upon the subject "coal tar" by Mr. W. M. Jones, F.C.S., in the Bold-street Wesleyan Schoolroom. The Rev. G. D. Mason presided. The lecturer said coal tar was a by-product which is produced in the manufacture of coal gas. When coal gas was in its infancy, and science had not found any use for the coal tar which was produced in its mantifacture, very large Sums were paid by the gas works' proprietors to anyone who would take this great nuisance away. He then went on to describe how the different sub- stances were got from coal tar, and their uses in the commerce of this country. The lecture was illus- trated by a number of experiments, and after the lecture the audience were invited to make an inspection of a number orsubstances which were exhibited, end which had been manufactured from coal tar. On Monday night, as a farmer's cart laden with grain was emerging from the Eagle and Child entry, into Bridge-street, it aim, st ran into one of the 'buses belonging to the Warrington Carriage Com- pany, which was coming from the direction of Market Gate. In order to avoid a collision the driver of the cart pulled up sharply, with the result that the cart, being heavily weighted at the back, tipped up, carrying the horse into the air. There were on the cart, besides the driver, a woman with a baby in her arms and a little girl. All were thrown out and buried underneath the grain. The woman received an ugly gash on the head, but the children and the driver escaped uninjured. A number of men set to wotk to extricate the horse, which was in imminent risk of being strangled. In his struggles he broke one of the shafts, which made its position still more painful. Ultimately, however, the cart was righted, and the poor animal was released from the harness. CONCERT IN ST. THE SHOCKING RAILWAY ACCIDENT NEAR WARRI§GTON.

An inquest on the body of John Dobson, a plate- layer, who was killed on Friday afterniaon near Warrington by an express train, was held on Satur- day at the Walton Arms, Walton, before Mr. Worsley, coroner. Mr. Jones, inspector, appeared on behalf of the Railway Company.—The first wit- ness called was John James Dobson,the father, who said that he was a platelayer. He was with the deceased on Friday afternoon at 1.50 on the line between Acton Grange Viaduct and Walton New Junction. There were four of them return- ing to work after dinner. The deceved, who had gone to fetch something he had left behind before he went for his dinner, would be from 40 to 50 yards away from them. Seeing the Manchester express coming, witness shouted to the deceased to beware, and he replied, " A.ll right." He saw the engine strike the deceased and cut off his head and one of his legs. The deceased, who was quite sober, had been a platelayer for about 29 years. The driver of the train whistled at about the same time that witness gave the alarm, and drew up as soon as possible.—Richard Forster, platelayer, Moore, gave corroborative evidence.—Theii driver of the Great Western express train, Henry Smith, was also called. He stated that he was on the look out and saw the accident. He pulled up as soon as possible. The man was dead when picked up. The Coroner, in summing up the evidence to the jury, said the driver was entirely free from blame. His conduct was commendable inasmuch as he stopped the train immediately, hoping that, if the man was alive,he might be taken on to the nearest railway station and thence to a hospita'.—The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death."

1894.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON. SERMON TO WORKINGMEN BY THE REV. D. W.

ROBERTS.

A service for workingmen was held at the Baptist chapel, Golhorne-street,on Sunday evening, the preacher being the pastor,the Rev. D. W. Roberts. The re.v.gentle- man took as his text Phil. iv. c. 2v. In the course of his address he said that life was, in one sense, a battlefield, where the battle for supremacy and for liberty had been, and was being continually, fought, whether by nation or individual. Humanly speaking, the grand crisis in the history of the world was the cantest between freedom and despotism,at the plain of Marathon ,where the Greeks saved Europe from falling under the rule of the Persian despot. Another crisis was when England was delivered from the spirit of bieotry and tyranny of Philip II ef 'Spain, by the defeat of the renowned Armada. The battle of Waterloo also freed Europe from the despotism of Napoleon. The last effectual blow to slavery was e..iyen at the great American civil war. Great as those conquests had been, and great as had been the liberty and freedom following them, a greater deliverance was wrought by the world's redeemer on Calvary 1900 years ago. That would be their one theme throughout eter- nity. Never was a greater battle. fought. It was be- tween light and darkness, life and death, salvation and sin, and man might now rejoice in the liberty where- with Christ had made him free. Next to the battle which Jesus Christ had fought was the battle which men were fighting for the liberty of the human race. They often sang, " Britons never shall be slaves," but was that so ? Was he not a slave to capital, and circumstances over which he had no control? It was not by his free-will that the working men of to-day worked twelve or four- ten hours, took competitive wage, lived ie poor tene- ments at high rents, &c. They were struggling to free themselves by means of strikes,protest, organisation,anci even desperation of physical violence. The Apostle Paul in the text condemned exclusive selfishness, and incul- cated Christian sympathy and generosity. He showed that only as they were actuated by those influencing motives they "could esteem others better than them- selves," and " look on the things of others." They were not to consult each his own interest but cherish mutual sympathy, and engage in mutual co-operation. They had to interest themselves in the concerns of others—the employer for the employe and the employe for the em- ployer—in Christian love and sympathy. He was not regardless of the fact that workingmen were for the most part regardless as to the interests of their masters, and were therefore selfish for the tendency was to consider one's own sensual gratification to the extent that work was neglected,and even when done was done in such a careless manner that it would not bear the test, and, consequently, the em- ployer sufferei, and the workman must naturally do so in time. He had never heard of an instance of a man fail- ing who studied the interests of his employers as if they were his own. He was afraid that there was too much selfishness and callousness to the feelings of others in the assertion of one's rights. There were thousands of workingmen who believed in an" Eight Hour " Bill (and he, the speaker, did for some trades), and they would attend a demonstration in favour of the Bill on a Saturday afternoon, and denounce in the bitterest of terms the unfeeling conduct of their masters. But where did they find such after nine o'clock in the evening? See them making purchases at shops which they could have done quite as well in the earlier part of the day. By doing it after nine o'clock at night they debarred the poor assistants who had been running about all the day, not only from the chance of an eight hour day, but in SOM3 cases, even from a twelve or fourteen hour day. Who kept the different places or shops open for trade on the Sabbath Day, and thus deprived others of a day of rest? How the workingman would frown and agitate if he himself were deprived of enjoying the Sabbath. Yet he deprived others, for if he did not buy the shop would not open. Then there were those who held up their hands in holy horror at the iniquities of the sweat- ing system, and yet went from shop to shop where there was a chance of saving a penny, knowing full well that the extra cheapness of an article could only be got by an extra pinch of the already underpaid worker. It might be that they were now wearing a pair of boots,or an article of clothing made by an ill-fed workman, a stunted hollow-eyed girl, or a poor sewing woman, who had had to pawn her shawl to get bread for herself and her children. Let them be done with such indifference and selfishness, and when they asserted their own rights let them "look on the things of others," and feel for them.

FORELGN COMPETITION IN THE IRON TRADE.

A WARRINGTON MANUFACTURER'S OPINION' At, the Iron and Steel Trade Conference in Glasgow on Tuesday, Mr. Jeremiah Lyon (London) sent a paper on the special conditions affecting our iron trade with India. The paper stated that the British iron manufacturer was being gradually elbowed out of the Indian market by the Belgian and German competitors. He asked why it was to-day that iron could be shipped by steamer from Antwerp to Bom- bay or Calcutta at 10s. per ton, while from London or Liverpool it was 15s. 9d. per ton. So far as London was concerned, the carrying trade to Bom- bay, Madras, and C-lcutta was exclusively in the hands of two powerful cottp-2..nies, both of whom were heavily subsidised, the one by the Government and the other by the Government of India. For some reason the Penin- sular and Oriental Company was so desirous of helping the Belgian iron trade that it sent its own steamers to load in Antwerp, and would brine' iron from there to London and thence to Bombay or Calcutta for 10s. per ton. He wished the Association to grasp that astound- ing fact. The English iron trade was further b 3rdened by tha higher rates charged by our railway companies, as comphred with those charged in Belgium. If the English railways charged relatively 'the same as the Belgian the rate from Birmingham to Birkenhead would be 4s. Id., as against 123. 6d. Hence the Birmingham iron manufacturer stood at a disadvantage of 83. 6d. per ton as compared with the Belgian competitor.— Mr. Monks (Warrington) said that he had been i 1 India recently, and at Bombay when he looked for English bar iron it was like looking for a needle in a stack of hay. He saw tons and tons of iron which had been made in Germany and Belgium. Then in Germany he had found that the head steel roller got Bs. per day, whereas in this country he got from El to £2 per day. With reference to the railway rates, the understandinc, arrived at, was that the iron and steel rates should not be increased. So far as his experience went, there was no attempt made to increase the rates, but the railway companies were reading certain clauses in such a way as would have the inevitable result of increasing the rates.—Mr. Wilson believed that the Indian people would rather trade with the English makers than the Belgians and Germans if prices would allow thern.—Mr. Monks knew tlft they would be willing to pay ss. per ton more for English iron rather than pay the same to the foreigners. He was .convinced of this, that the manufacturers had less to fear at the hands of the merchants than they had at the hands of the foreign makers.— Mr. Hopwood wished Mr. Monks to tell them if he did not see iron stamped with the words " made in Ger- man?" which had been made in Britain.—Mr. Monks replied that the Trade Marks Act was enforced in India. as well as in England, and what Mr. Hopwood asked could not be seen.—Mr. Hopwood then asked if Mr. Monk's expert knowledge did not detect foreign iron stamped with the English mark.—Mr. Monks replied no ! but a friend of his had once seen iron marked " Lancashire Swedish." (Laughter.) Mr. Fawcett wished to know whether iron and steel made on the Continent was better or worse than the same articles manufactured in Britain. He understood that some of the goods made in Germany and other parts were better finished than those made here. —Mr. Monks said so far as quality was concerned Belgian iron was not equal to ordinary Staffordshire iron.—rhe president was of opinion that whilst the Peninsular and Oriental Company was perfectly justified to do what it liked, that did not justify them in taking a subsidy from our Government, and then,whilst enjoy- ing that subsidy, to use it for the purprse of cutting the people whose Government paid that sulaidy. (Ap- plause.)

WARRINGTON TRAINING COLLEGE.

OPENING OF NEW BUILDINGS.

On Friday afternoon last week, notwithstanding the steady downpour of rain, there was a large attendance at the Training College to witness the opening of the new buildings which have been erect- ed there. The additions include an art room,recrea- tion room, additional dormitories and class rooms, and the enlargement of the dining-room and scullery. These extensive additions enable ,the pro- moters to increase the number of students by 29, and they have been carried out with the permission of the Education Department. The cost of the alterations and fnrnishing has amounted to £4,5C03 of which £3,000 hes been already raised. Mr. Dolan, of Warrington, has been the contractor, and Messrs. S. E. and R. Johnson (Bridge-st.) had charge of the furnishing arrangements, Amongst those present at the opening ceremony were Mrs. Beamont, the Very Rev. Dr. Maclure (Dean of Manchester), Very Rev. Arch- deacon Barber, of Chester; Rev. Canons Nunn, Holme, Scott, Penrhvn, BluiLlells,Rev, F. W. Willis (rector),and Mrs. Willi., Revs. Morley Stevenson (Principal), E. C. E. Carleton, G. B. Thurston, J. G. Evans, H. R. Wilde, D. Percival. A. A. Markham, J. Bell-Cox, Mr. H. E. Oakley (ELM. Inspector), Alderman Wright, Councillor Woods, Dr. White, Mr. J. S. Garland, and Mr. R. Johnson. 'The DEAN of MANCHESTER, in asking Mrs. Beamont to declare the buildings open, spoke of the generosity of the Duke of Westminster in contributing largely towards the erection of those buildings, and apologised for hi s absence that day. He also alluded to the honoured name of Belmont, and spoke of the interest Mrs. Bea mont took in that institution. Mrs. BEAMONT said she felt it a great honour arid pleasure to be there and to express the great interest she had towards that college. She congratulated the exci/1.- lent principal and his excellent staff and committee upon the success of the college and heartily wished it to pros- per. She had great pleasure in saying that those rooms were now open.

A vote of thanks was passed to Mrs. Beamont for her presence, on the proposition of Archdeacon BARBER, seconded hv Canon PENRYN.

Miss EDITH BOYLA.NCE, senior student of the college, then stepped forward and presented Mrs. Rea.mont with a beautiful bouquet, the gift of Miss Goddard, the staff and student.

An adjournment was then made to the dining-room, when the Dean of Manchester occupied the chair and an address was delivered by Her Majesty's Inspector (Mr. 0 _keley). He referred to the necessary imp rovements which had been carried out and made some observations on the examinations he had conducted th ere during the week, and also spoke on educational matters generally. In conclusion he urged the students to live good and earnest lives that they might set themselves as a model to their pupils.

The Dean of MANCTIF,STER after apologising for the non-attendance of several gentlemen, spoke of the work of the College during the last ten years—which he des- cribed as an epoch in the history of the college—during which time it had had had for its principal the Rev. Morley Stevenson. Referring to the financial part of the scheme, he stated that they had still to raise £1,500. He proceeded to urge upon those present to do their utmost to clear off the liabilities still existing.

The Rev. MORLEY STEVENSON gave an account of all the improvements which had been carried out, stating that they had fl )W accommodation for 120 pupils in stead of 100 as formerly. It was the 50th year in the life of that College, and they had done something to celebrate their jubilee.

EARLESTOWN AND DISTRICT BUILDING

SOCIETY.

ANNUAL MEETING,

The annual general meeting of the members of this Society was held in the Earlest own district schoolroom on Thursday evening. Mr.F. Harwood, chairman of directors presided, and he was sup- ported by Mr. R. Davies, Mr. Kirkconnel (Warring- ton), Mr. Appleby (auditor), Mr. J.W. Emmett, and Mr. Chandley (secretary). The report of the Directors was as follows :—" The directors have pleasure in submitting this their thirty- first annual report and statement of accounts certified by the auditors, and congratulate the shardiolders on the continued prosperity of the Society. The profits for the year amount to £1,680 12s. id., which with the undivided balance from last year of £2,348 10s. Bd., shows a disposable amount of £4,029 23. 9J. From which it is proposed to recommend the payment of a dividend at the rate of 5 per cent, per annam, absorbing £l,BBB 3s. 4d., leaving a balance of undivided profit of £2,342 19s. sd. Four members have redeemed their property, representing repayment of capital to the amount of £1,549 03. 10d. Two lots of property only on hand. The number of members on books is 405, hold- ing 3,385 shares.

The Chairman said; they met that night on rather more favourable terms than they did last year. Then, if they remembered, they should have had to take from the reserve fund if they had paid more tan 44 per cit., but the directors felt a diffidence in doing that, and now they met them with a dividend of 5 per cent., but they had to take five guineas from the reserve fund, but he did not think they would find any fault with that, seeing that they had put £7O to the reserve fund last year. Last:year they had something like £5OOO in the bank, and he believed he told them then that there was a prospect of being able to get that out rather earlier ; and he was hippy to say they did so,and at the present time they Ipid reduced that £5,000 to something like £3OO. That was more favourable to the society,as it enabled them to get the money out on good interest, and on good property. He concluded by moving the adoption of the report and balance sheet. This was sec- onded and carried.

Mr. J. W. Emmett asked what amount of money was on the two lots of property they had on hand. The Chairman said it amounted to £1,103. Mr. R. Davies said they had here a bona fide dividend of 5 per cent.,which in these days was most remarkable. He was proud to see Mr. Emmett amongst them, as he was the senior trustee of that prosperous society. He had really not much to say about the society, but pleasant thoughts for the future sometimes crossed his mind. He was only too proud to meet them, and hoped he rnight be spared to meet them on many occasions, as it pleased him to come there year after year. (Ap- plause.) Mr. Emmet, might just remind their solicitor, Mr. Davies,that some two or thiee years ago lie did not speak quite so cheerfully about the prospects of building clubs and trade, as he did that night. He thought he gave them to understand that they ought to be satisfied, if they got 4 to 41 per cent., and he (Mr. Emmett) thought he told him that he thought they should live to see the day when they would get 5 per cent. They had lived to see that day, and he believed they should go on improv- ing, and not let other countries cut them out in any- thing. (Hear, hear.) He was very well ple3ed with the balance sheet, and he thought it was only fair to congratulate the chairman and directors of the Society, for they well deserv, d it. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Appleby (auditor), thought there was ev ry cause for congratulation on the part of the society, when it was receiving 5 per cent on its mone.y, and felt sure that money was safely guarded. Since they last met there had been a new Act passed affecting building societies, and which came into operation on the Ist of January next year. He did not know that it would affect them in any way, except on the lines that Mr. Emmett had asked the qw•stion on. He had asked how much money was owing on the properties on hand. That would have to be stated in their balance sheets in future. Another thing was that they would I: lye to have one professional man as auditcr. Another was that they could not advancQ money on second mortgages. He concluded by congratulating them upon the sound state of the Society.

ELECTION OF OFFICEP.S.

The election of officers was then proceeded with and resulted as follows :—Direetors Mr. F. Harwood (presi- dent) Messrs. W. Gilmour, T. Haselden, J. Barr, J. Cook J. Walker, Joseph Emmett, N. Slinn, W. H. Bailey, B. Harrison, surveyor, Mr. E. Bunting. Auditors, Mr. T. Thompson and Mr. W. Appleby. Stewards, Messrs. T. Ormand and J. Gooding. The Chairman said he would like to refer to the loss they had sustained by the death of Mr. John Elliott, their surveyor. He was one of the founders of the society, and had been connected with it, over 30 years, and he t2lieved there was only one other gentleman left as a founder of the society, and that was Mr. B. Walker.

A vote of thanks to the Chairman on the motion of Mr. Emmett, seconded by Mr. Whitley, concluded the meeting.

HALTON.

A MARRIAGE THAT PROVED A FAILIIRE.—On Monday at the Runcorn Petty Sessions before Messrs. James Handley and Samuel Taylor, a summons was heard in which Mary Dugdale charged her husband, George Dug- dale, with having assaulted her on the 20th October at Halton. She sought a separation and in support of her application said that on the evening of the 20th of Outober her husband began immediately after entering the house to use the most filthy language tocvares her in the presence of his little children. The house was very comfoJtable, and he wanted for nothing. He had not been provoked, and complainant did not know of any reason why he should treat her so. He afterwards pulled her about the room by her hair, and pushed her outside the house. Her arms were black Pi a result of his abuse, while drag- ging her about the home. Ev had been sepArated from her husband for twelve months, but consented again to live with him two years ago, upon the strength of his promises to treat her kindly. The last se- paration order she obtained stipulated that her husband should pay her 15s. pee week She then had four children but now she had six. Her husband earned 30s. per week and worked on th tug " Liberator."—Mr. Linaker, who represented the defendant, contended that there had been no assault committed. The defendant was fined 55. and 153. 6d. costs, and an order of separation was granted, the de- fendant, to pay his wife 16s. per week towards th 4 maintenance of his wife and children.

DRITNIE ON His OWN LICENSED PREMISES.—At the Run- corn Petty S- -ions, on Monday, before Messrs. James Handley and Samuel Taylor, John Simpson, licencee of the Castle Flot^l,Halton,was summoned for being drunk on those premiK lon the 23rd Octot 2r. P.C. Juett de- posed to finding the defendant in the bar of the hotel named. He was in a drunken condition, and was try- ing to help him. _if to some liquor. His wife refused to let him have any, and requested h'm to leave the house. This he would not do, and when witness spoke to him he replied that he would see a medical man as to his condition. On reaching the threshold of the house, de- fendant asked witness to look over the case. Supt. Atherton remarked that Mr. Allen, who was present, might enlighten the magistrates as to how mat- ters stood. Mr. Allen explained that the defend- ant had been summoned by his wife for assault. A cross-summons had been issued, and the hearing had been adjourned until the next Sessions, with the view of getting an agreement of separation. It was contemplated that the license, which was in the name of the defendant, should be transferred to Mrs. Simpson.—Defendant emphatically denied that he was drunk and then added that he was 70 years of ago and had never before been in a police court upon such a charge.—He was fined 10s. and costs, or 14 days.

The prices charged for the goods are enormous, without any regard for quality, the agreements are one-sided, being drawn with a cuteness which would do credit to a money lender, full of despotic and arbitrary restrictions, suchas not to remove the mark on the furniture, not to remove the furniture, not to wear the furniture. At all times to allow the mere, naries to inspect the furniture. To pay the instal- mcnts regularly, and if oneis missed to forfeit the furniture and all the moneys you may have paid, that is to say, although you might have paid £l9 on £2 worth of furniture, but missed the last payment by a single day, the vultures could empty your house and stick to the £l9 paid, as well as the furniture. WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL. A meeting of this Council was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening. In the absence of the Mayor and the Deputy-Mayor, Alderman Harrison was elected to preside. The other members present were Aldermen Blackhurst, Sutton, Hutchinson, Bolton; Councillors J. Fairclough, Roberts, Green- ing, Hill, Smethurst, Bennett, Woods, Skelton, Shill- cock, Longton, R. T. Fairclough, Evans, shaw, %ilkinson, Beaman, Garner, Baird, .Plumpton, Davies, Heaton, Hepherd, Cannell ; Messrs. Whittle (town clerk), and Longden (borough surveyor). THE RESIGNATION OF COUNCILLOR FLOOD. The CHAIRMAN stated that the resignation of Coun- cillor Flood had been received. As the term of Coun- cillor Flood's office terminated the next day it was not necessary to give the usual notice for tilling up the vacancy. The resignation was accepted. THE PROSECUTIONS FOR SUNDAY SHAVING. Councillor Serew asked the Chairman of the Watch Committee " what course they intended to pursue after the recent prosecution of certain persons under the Lord's Day Act, and for the amount of cost incurred (if any), and whether it is intended to extend such prose- cutions to others carrying on Sunday trade." Councillor GREENING asked if the question was in order as the prosecutions had not been made by the Watch Committee. Councillor SHAW said he only wanted to know whether there ware any costs incurred in connection with the prosecutions. The CHAIRMAN: No costs whatever have been incurred by the Watch Committee: Councillor SHAW then proceeded to read an extract from the Lord's Day Act, but was interrupted by Councillor ROBERTS, who asked for the ruling of the Town Clerk as to whether Councillor Shaw was in order. Hitherto he had ruled that a member having given notice of a question could only ask that question and no more. The CHAIRMAN: That is what I have always under- stood. The TOWN CLERK replied that a member could explain his question, but no discussion could arise on it. Councillor SHAW, after reading from the Act, con- tended that it did not even touch upon the " quality or position "of a barber. Alderman SUTTON asked that time should not be wasted. It seemed to him that the question had already been answered, and therefore there was no necessity for further talk about it. Councillor SHAW : I only wanted to know whether there would be any more prosecutions. Ihe CHAIRMAN said he was prepared to answer the question positively. The prosecutions were not insti- tuted by the Watch Committee nor by the police ,but by the Hairdressers' Aesociation. Councillor SIAM' afterwards gave notice to move at the next meeting that the question be referred to the Watch Committee for consideration. THE PRINTING CONTRACT. At a meeting of the Finance and Estate Committee it was resolved that the tender of Messrs. Mackie and Co., Limited, for the printing and stationery required by the I Corporation for the next twelve months he accepted. The confirmation of the mieutes wee moved imy Coun- cillor HEPHERD, seconded by Councillor JAMES FAIR- CLOUGH. Councillor LoNoTori said he should like to know whether the tender of Messrs. Mackie and Co. for print- ins, was advertised for, and whether it was not the fact that the prices were higher in every respect this year than they had ever been? Councillor JAMES FAIRCLOUGH said the tender was advera,ed for in the usual manner, and there was no tender from anyone but Messrs. Mackie and Co. There was a sub-committee formed to interview Mesers.Mackie and Co., to arrange the whole matter with them, and they were quite satisfied with the result. The subject then dropped, and the minutes were passed. THE LAYING OUT OF STREETS AND THE ADOPTION OF PLANS. Alderman SUTTON proposed, and Aderman BLACK- HURST seconded, the adoption of the minutes of the Street Improvement Committee which proposed the adoption of several plans. Councillor BENNETT oomplained that whore two houses had been built at the corner of Gorsey-lane and Padgate- lane there was not a width of 40 feet. He further re- marked that the streets between the Parish Church and Padgate-lane were wofully mismanaged, and were not laid out in accordance with any steady principle or even a principle of• common geometry. In the words of Elijah Gough, "Thee aimed at nothing, and succeeded beyond all expectation." (Laughter.) Councillor WOODS understood that the two houses in Gorsey-lane were in course of erection before the plans were sanctioned by the committee. If that was true it was a breach of the bylaws, and should not be allowed in any case, more particularly in houses of that class. (Hear, hear.) Councillor SHAW said he was building those two houses himself, and he was able to Say that the street was over 40 feet wide. Alderman SUTTON suggested that notice should be given of questions of thiskind,so that they might be able to give proper and fair answers to them. The plans now in question came before the committee in the regular way, and he could say that the streets were 40 feet wide, and at one part more than that. There was not a single case, either in that neighbourhood or any other, where the bylaws had not been observed. If anybody began to erect property before obtaining the sanction, of the committee, it was upon his own responsibility,and if the houses should happen to be not in accordance with the bylaws he would have to pull them down. The minutes were then confirmed. HEALTH COMMITTEE.-INSANITARY DWELLINGS. Alderman Sterroxe moved, and Councillor SKELTON , seconded, the adoption of this Committee's minutes which contained the following resolution: "That the Town Clerk be instructed to take the necessary proceed- ings under the Housing of the Working Classes Act, 1890. for the closing of the undermentioned houses, all I of which have been reported by the Medical Officer of Health to be unfit for human habit ,tion :-1, 2,3, 4,5, 6,7, Arnold's Yard, Haydock-st. ; 1,2, 3,4, 5,6, Back Haydock-st. ;1, 2. 3, Buckley's Yard, Hayclock-st. ; 4, 5,6; Buckley's Yard, Haydock-st. ; 7,8, Buckley's Yard, Haydock-et. ; 1, 2. 3,5, 7. 9, Boardman's Court, Haydock-st. ; 1,2, 50, 52, 51, 56, 58. 60, Gaskell's Yard, Golborne-st. ; 37, Golborne-st. ; 1, Cambria Place ;• 24, Gelborne-st. ' • 3, Cambria Place; 2, 4,6, 8, William-st. ; 10, 12. 14, 16, 18, William-st. •, 8, 6. 4,2, Back William- st. ; 49, School Brow; 13, 15, Buckley-st., Winwick- road." Councillor HILL moved as an amendment that the minutes pass with the exception of the ahove resolution. He said that a number of the owners of those houses had received notice that their houses were in an insani- tary condition before they had a chance to put them in a sanitary condition. He thought the honest thing to do was instead of sending that vague notice they ought to tell the owners what they must do. He did not be- lieve that the property owners liked to have their pro- perty in an insanitary state. Alderman SUTTON: Are they your property ? Councillor HILL : That's my business. Alderman SUTTON : Perhaps You will tell es. Councillor Then : There are 55 houses,and there were 42 in September, a total of 97 houses. Whore are you going to find room for those families in the town? The CHAIRMAN : I think you are precluded from dis- cussing this matter. Councillor ROBERTS asked the Town Clerk whether if a member of the Council takes part in a discussion in which he had a personal interest, he was not liable to prosecution. The TOWN CLERK : The law is that anyone who has a pecuniary interest is precluded from discussing it. It is a direct breach of the law to discuss it. Councillor SMETHURST said that he would move the amendment as Councillor Bill could not.. The action of the committee was literally turning the families into the street. Alderman SUTTON rose to a point of order. Were they not out of order in discussing the question as they wers bound to serve notice under time Act? The TOWN CLERK said the Health Committee was appointed by the Council to carry out the Act,and that, therefore, they had to deal with the cases reported to ! them. Councillor ROBERTS observed that the notice which was issued was required to be issued by law. The pre- sent resolution was in the words of the Act, and they I simple meant that notice was given to the people who owned tit houses to put them in better condition. It was onl• a preliminary notice to the owners, and if the ownerr came before the committee they would no doubt be informed of what was required. If the property were put in a sanitary condition the notice fell to the ground and is of no further effect, hut. if the houses were incap- able of repair that was the first notice which the con). mittee took towards absolutely closing the property. Alderman SUTTON said that the committee were bound to act upon the report of the Medical Officer of Health, and lie promised that the property owners, if they came before the committee, would receive every considera- tion. The minutes were then passed. PAVING AND SEWERAGE COMMITTEE. Alderman WRIGHT moved the adoption of this Com- mittee's minutes, which contained the following tender: -That the tender of Mr. John Melbourne to supply 50 cast-iron ventilator covers at £34, and the tender of Mr. John Chorley to supply 50 ditto at £34, he accepted. Councillor DAVIES drew attention to the condition of

JOHN ASHTON, Clerk to the Guardians. Runcorn, Ist November, 1894.
